Table 2
Teams: Psychological Safety, Collective Intelligence, and Effective Team Norms
1. Q: What is the central argument of Chapter 2, “Teams: Psychological Safety at Google and Saturday Night Live”?
A: Show answer
The chapter argues that team effectiveness depends not only on the people selected but also on the norms governing how they work together. Talented individuals can underperform when members compete for status, suppress questions, hide mistakes, or dominate discussion. Teams make better use of their combined capabilities when members can contribute, disagree, ask for help, and acknowledge uncertainty without fear of humiliation. The chapter illustrates this through Google’s Project Aristotle, hospital teams studied by Amy Edmondson, and the original Saturday Night Live team. Its central theme is that psychological safety, balanced participation, social sensitivity, and clear working norms help turn individual ability into collective performance.
Study instruction:
- "Explain why team interaction norms matter alongside individual skill, using examples from at least two chapter cases."
Key point:
- The central issue is how people work together, not only who is on the team.
- Psychological safety helps members contribute, learn, and challenge ideas constructively.
Source links:
- Google: The Five Dynamics of Effective Teams
- Harvard: Psychological Safety and Learning Behavior in Work Teams
- Woolley et al.: Evidence for a Collective Intelligence Factor
2. Q: What was Google’s Project Aristotle designed to investigate?
A: Show answer
Project Aristotle was an internal Google research initiative intended to understand why some teams worked effectively while others did not. Researchers examined academic literature, interviewed employees, surveyed teams, and compared team practices and performance. They initially explored whether an ideal combination of personalities, backgrounds, skills, or experience could explain success. Their data did not reveal a reliable formula based on team composition alone. Attention then shifted toward group norms: shared, often unwritten expectations about participation, listening, disagreement, support, and decision-making. The project therefore reframed the question from finding the perfect mix of people to understanding the conditions that allow a group to perform well.
Study instruction:
- "Describe the research question, the evidence-gathering methods, and the shift from composition to norms."
Key point:
- Project Aristotle examined why some Google teams were more effective than others.
- Its key shift was from the composition of teams to the norms shaping interactions.
Source links:
- Google: The Five Dynamics of Effective Teams
3. Q: Why did Project Aristotle fail to identify one universally successful personality mix?
A: Show answer
The researchers observed effective teams with very different compositions and working styles. Some teams socialized outside work, while others did not; some preferred strong leadership, while others used more distributed responsibility. Groups with similar membership could also perform differently. These findings made it difficult to identify a universal combination of extroversion, introversion, expertise, or background that predicted success. The result does not mean individual skills are irrelevant. A team still needs the competence required for its task, but composition alone cannot explain whether members share information, coordinate work, challenge assumptions, and learn from mistakes. The way people interact affects whether individual talent becomes useful collective output.
Study instruction:
- "Distinguish the conclusion that composition alone is insufficient from the claim that expertise does not matter."
Key point:
- No simple personality formula guarantees team effectiveness.
- Individual capability must be converted into coordinated group behavior.
Source links:
- Google: The Five Dynamics of Effective Teams
- Woolley et al.: Evidence for a Collective Intelligence Factor
4. Q: What are group norms, and how do they influence a team's performance?
A: Show answer
Group norms are shared expectations and unwritten rules that shape behavior inside a team. They influence who speaks, how people respond to disagreement, whether questions are welcomed, how errors are handled, and whether members help one another. Some norms are explicit, such as a rule that every person contributes before a decision. Others are implicit, such as an expectation that junior staff should not question a senior manager. Norms develop through repeated interactions and are reinforced by what leaders tolerate or reward. A norm that encourages questions can surface problems early; a norm that rewards silence can hide risks. Norms therefore determine how much of the team's knowledge and capability is actually used.
Study instruction:
- "Identify three implicit norms in a real team and explain how each affects participation or delivery."
Key point:
- Norms are the repeated behavioral expectations that govern team interaction.
- They can support learning and coordination or reinforce silence and dysfunction.
Source links:
- Harvard: Psychological Safety and Learning Behavior in Work Teams
- Google: The Five Dynamics of Effective Teams
5. Q: Why did Julia Rozovsky experience different dynamics in her Yale study group and case-competition team?
A: Show answer
The groups contained capable students, but they developed different norms. In the study group, members competed for authority, interrupted each other, pre-emptively assigned tasks, and criticized ideas in ways that made Julia feel she had to prove herself. In the case-competition team, members welcomed suggestions, explored unusual ideas before rejecting them, divided work, and allowed different people to lead. Even an impractical suggestion could be examined rather than dismissed immediately. The contrasting experiences showed that similar people can behave differently under different group expectations. The team's atmosphere was not simply a product of individual personalities; it was shaped by the behaviors the group repeatedly accepted.
Study instruction:
- "Compare the two teams and list observable behaviors that contributed to their different climates."
Key point:
- Similar individuals can form teams with very different interaction patterns.
- Team culture is demonstrated by repeated behavior, not by friendly intentions alone.
Source links:
- Google: The Five Dynamics of Effective Teams
- Harvard: Psychological Safety and Learning Behavior in Work Teams
6. Q: What is psychological safety in a team?
A: Show answer
Psychological safety is a shared belief that the team is safe for interpersonal risk-taking. Members feel able to ask questions, request help, admit an error, express uncertainty, challenge a proposal, or offer a new idea without being humiliated or punished simply for speaking. The concept concerns the team's interpersonal climate, not a promise that every contribution will be accepted. A psychologically safe team can still reject weak proposals, enforce professional standards, and hold people accountable. Its members can raise a concern and expect it to be considered fairly. Edmondson’s research connected psychological safety with learning behaviors such as seeking feedback, discussing errors, and asking for assistance.
Study instruction:
- "Memorize the definition and provide four workplace examples of interpersonal risk-taking."
Key point:
- Psychological safety is a shared belief about the consequences of speaking up.
- It enables candor and learning; it does not remove standards or accountability.
Source links:
- Harvard: Psychological Safety and Learning Behavior in Work Teams
7. Q: Does psychological safety mean that everyone must feel comfortable or agree with one another?
A: Show answer
No. Psychological safety is not the absence of tension, difficult feedback, or disagreement. It means people can raise relevant issues and challenge ideas without being personally attacked or humiliated. A safe team may have vigorous debate, reject a proposal, and address poor performance while maintaining respect. Superficial harmony can be unsafe when members believe they must remain silent to avoid upsetting a leader. The useful distinction is between challenging an idea and attacking the person who expressed it. Teams should encourage evidence-based disagreement while requiring participants to listen, explain their reasoning, and respect appropriate decision processes.
Study instruction:
- "Rewrite a personal criticism as a clear challenge to an idea, assumption, or piece of evidence."
Key point:
- Safety supports honest disagreement rather than suppressing it.
- Respectful challenge focuses on reasoning and evidence, not personal worth.
Source links:
- Harvard: Psychological Safety and Learning Behavior in Work Teams
- Google: The Five Dynamics of Effective Teams
8. Q: How is psychological safety different from interpersonal trust?
A: Show answer
Interpersonal trust concerns the expectation that another person will behave reliably or with goodwill. Psychological safety concerns a shared belief about whether members can take interpersonal risks within the team. The ideas overlap, but they are not identical. An employee might trust a colleague's technical expertise yet feel unable to question that colleague in a meeting. A team may also allow candid debate without its members being close friends. Psychological safety is therefore assessed by looking at whether members can ask, challenge, disclose, and learn without disproportionate social punishment. Trust can support the climate, but friendship or individual trust alone does not establish safe group norms.
Study instruction:
- "Construct an example where a person trusts a colleague but still feels unsafe challenging that colleague publicly."
Key point:
- Trust and psychological safety are related but distinct.
- Psychological safety concerns the shared climate for speaking and learning.
Source links:
- Harvard: Psychological Safety and Learning Behavior in Work Teams
9. Q: What kinds of interpersonal risks do people take when they speak up at work?
A: Show answer
Interpersonal risks include admitting a mistake, asking a question that may reveal a knowledge gap, disagreeing with a senior person, proposing an untested idea, requesting help, reporting a near miss, or explaining that a deadline is unrealistic. These actions may threaten a person's reputation or relationships when the group responds with ridicule, exclusion, blame, or retaliation. A psychologically safer climate reduces the expected social penalty for reasonable, work-related candor. For example, a finance analyst who questions an unsupported assumption or a nurse who reports an error provides information the organization needs. Leaders should encourage such disclosure while still distinguishing honest reporting from careless or deliberate misconduct.
Study instruction:
- "List five interpersonal risks in your workplace and identify a response that would encourage each contribution."
Key point:
- Speaking up, asking for help, and admitting errors are interpersonal risks.
- Constructive responses encourage candor while retaining professional standards.
Source links:
- Harvard: Psychological Safety and Learning Behavior in Work Teams
10. Q: What did Amy Edmondson's hospital research reveal about team cohesion and reported errors?
A: Show answer
Edmondson initially expected hospital units with stronger teamwork to have fewer errors. Instead, the units with stronger cohesion appeared to report more errors. Further analysis showed that some teams were more comfortable disclosing mistakes, while staff in other units feared that errors would be held against them. The apparent contradiction was therefore partly a reporting effect: stronger teams could have higher reported error counts because they were more open about mistakes. The case highlights the need to distinguish the actual frequency of errors from the frequency with which errors are detected and reported. Open reporting gives organizations a better opportunity to investigate causes and improve practice.
Study instruction:
- "Explain why the reported error count may differ from the actual underlying error rate."
Key point:
- More reported errors can reflect greater willingness to disclose mistakes.
- Incident counts must be interpreted in light of reporting behavior and detection.
Source links:
- Edmondson: Learning from Mistakes Is Easier Said Than Done
- Harvard: Psychological Safety and Learning Behavior in Work Teams
11. Q: Why can a cohesive team still have low psychological safety?
A: Show answer
Cohesion describes how connected or united members feel; it does not prove they can challenge decisions or admit mistakes safely. A team may be proud of its identity, maintain a supportive social environment, and still discourage questions that could threaten its reputation or authority structure. In the hospital example, some cohesive staff feared that admitting an error would expose them to criticism. Loyalty can become restrictive when members believe protecting the group's image is more important than reporting a problem. Effective teams need relationships that support cooperation as well as norms that permit candid disclosure, disagreement, and correction.
Study instruction:
- "Assess a cohesive group for evidence of open dissent, error reporting, help-seeking, and fair responses to criticism."
Key point:
- Cohesion and psychological safety are not interchangeable.
- Loyalty can suppress learning when honesty is treated as disloyalty.
Source links:
- Edmondson: Learning from Mistakes Is Easier Said Than Done
- Harvard: Psychological Safety and Learning Behavior in Work Teams
12. Q: What is the difference between a learning-oriented response to an error and a blame-oriented response?
A: Show answer
A learning-oriented response establishes what happened, contains immediate risk, gathers evidence, identifies contributing conditions, and determines how recurrence can be reduced. A blame-oriented response focuses prematurely on identifying someone to punish, which can encourage concealment and incomplete reporting. A learning culture does not excuse reckless conduct, deliberate dishonesty, or repeated disregard of controls. Accountability remains necessary, but it should follow a fair examination of the facts, responsibilities, competence, process design, and intent. The organization should distinguish honest error from misconduct, take proportionate action, and use the incident to improve both individual practice and the system in which the work occurred.
Study instruction:
- "Design an error-review process that combines immediate containment, fact-finding, fair accountability, and corrective action."
Key point:
- Learning requires candid reporting and a fair investigation.
- Psychological safety does not eliminate responsibility for misconduct or repeated negligence.
Source links:
- Edmondson: Learning from Mistakes Is Easier Said Than Done
- Harvard: Psychological Safety and Learning Behavior in Work Teams
13. Q: What made the original Saturday Night Live team effective despite internal competition and conflict?
A: Show answer
The original SNL team was not free from tension. Members competed for airtime, criticized one another's work, formed alliances, and sometimes had personal disagreements. Yet they continued to offer ideas because the team norms allowed people to take creative risks and remain heard. Lorne Michaels encouraged pitches, noticed who had not contributed, and showed sensitivity to how members responded. The team did not succeed because everyone agreed or became selfless. It succeeded because competition and criticism were balanced by enough interpersonal safety for members to keep contributing and collaborating. The example shows that creative conflict can coexist with a productive team climate.
Study instruction:
- "Explain how the SNL case challenges the idea that an effective team must be conflict-free."
Key point:
- Psychological safety can coexist with creative competition and disagreement.
- The key is whether people remain able to contribute and be heard.
Source links:
- Google: The Five Dynamics of Effective Teams
- Harvard: Psychological Safety and Learning Behavior in Work Teams
14. Q: Why does the SNL example show that team members do not have to be close friends?
A: Show answer
The original SNL team included people with different personalities, ambitions, and relationships; some were not particularly close outside work. Their effectiveness relied more on professional interaction norms than on universal friendship. Members could criticize a sketch or compete for limited airtime while still allowing each other to contribute and continue working together. This shows that a team does not need forced intimacy to create psychological safety. It needs respect, social awareness, clear expectations, and responses that do not turn every disagreement into a personal rupture. Friendship may help cooperation, but it is not a substitute for fair participation and dependable work practices.
Study instruction:
- "Identify behaviors that support collaboration among colleagues who are not personal friends."
Key point:
- Friendship is not a prerequisite for psychologically safe teamwork.
- Respectful interaction and dependable norms are more operationally important than forced closeness.
Source links:
- Harvard: Psychological Safety and Learning Behavior in Work Teams
- Google: The Five Dynamics of Effective Teams
15. Q: How can a team combine psychological safety with rigorous disagreement?
A: Show answer
A team should make it safe to express a view while requiring members to examine the evidence and consequences behind it. Leaders can invite dissenting perspectives, separate criticism of an idea from criticism of its author, and ask participants to explain their reasoning. A member should be able to say that a plan is unsafe or unsupported without being labelled disloyal. In return, members should listen, respond to evidence, and understand how a decision will be made. Safety protects the ability to contribute; it does not guarantee that every proposal will be accepted or that debate continues indefinitely. Clear decision rules help translate discussion into action.
Study instruction:
- "Prepare meeting phrases that invite dissent while requiring claims to be supported by evidence."
Key point:
- Safety creates room for dissent; disciplined debate makes dissent useful.
- Challenge assumptions and evidence without attacking the person.
Source links:
- Harvard: Psychological Safety and Learning Behavior in Work Teams
- Google: The Five Dynamics of Effective Teams
16. Q: What is collective intelligence, and how does it differ from the intelligence of individual team members?
A: Show answer
Collective intelligence is a group's general ability to perform across a range of tasks. It is not simply the average IQ or the highest IQ of the people in the group. The chapter describes research in which groups completed different assignments requiring brainstorming, coordination, negotiation, and joint problem-solving. Some groups performed consistently well across tasks, while others struggled. The findings suggested a group-level performance factor that was not fully explained by individual intelligence. The implication is that interaction patterns can create an additional capability: a team can combine knowledge effectively or waste it through poor participation, listening, and coordination.
Study instruction:
- "Explain why collective intelligence is a group-level concept rather than a measure of the smartest individual."
Key point:
- Collective intelligence concerns performance across varied group tasks.
- Individual ability alone does not determine the quality of team performance.
Source links:
- Woolley et al.: Evidence for a Collective Intelligence Factor
17. Q: What did Woolley and colleagues investigate in their collective-intelligence study?
A: Show answer
The researchers recruited 699 participants, organized them into groups, and assigned tasks requiring different kinds of cooperation. The tasks included generating uses for a common object, coordinating a shared shopping exercise with different constraints, and reaching a decision in a case with competing interests. The researchers compared group performance across these tasks to assess whether a general collective-intelligence factor existed. They reported that group performance was correlated across different assignments and identified social processes associated with stronger results. The chapter uses the study to illustrate that team effectiveness is not reducible to the intelligence of the strongest member. Its findings should be interpreted within the study's sample and methods, not treated as a universal formula for every organization.
Study instruction:
- "Recall the sample, the range of tasks, and the purpose of comparing group performance across assignments."
Key point:
- The study tested group performance across several collaborative tasks.
- Its findings support the idea of a group-level capability.
Source links:
- Woolley et al.: Evidence for a Collective Intelligence Factor
18. Q: Why did high individual intelligence fail to guarantee high collective intelligence?
A: Show answer
Individual intelligence is useful but does not ensure that a group will exchange information, coordinate effort, consider alternative views, or use its members' knowledge. A team of highly capable people may underperform if one person dominates, members withhold concerns, disagreements become personal, or nobody notices when colleagues are confused. A group with less impressive individual test scores may do better when its members listen, contribute, adapt, and build on one another's ideas. This does not make expertise unimportant; the team still needs task-relevant competence. It means that social processes determine how effectively the available competence is combined and applied.
Study instruction:
- "Describe a technically strong team that fails because of poor coordination, then identify improvements to its interaction norms."
Key point:
- Individual intelligence cannot substitute for effective collaboration.
- Team processes determine whether members' knowledge is combined and applied.
Source links:
- Woolley et al.: Evidence for a Collective Intelligence Factor
19. Q: What is equality in the distribution of conversational turn-taking?
A: Show answer
Equality in conversational turn-taking means that participation is reasonably balanced across members over the relevant period. It does not require every person to speak for precisely the same number of seconds in each meeting. Different tasks may require different levels of contribution from particular specialists. The important pattern is that no individual or small subgroup monopolizes discussion while others remain persistently unheard. Balanced participation gives the team access to more information, helps expose overlooked risks, and reduces dependence on a narrow set of voices. Leaders should consider participation across meetings and tasks, not just count the number of comments in one session.
Study instruction:
- "Review a recent meeting and assess participation over the entire discussion rather than expecting identical speaking time."
Key point:
- Balanced participation helps teams access more of their members' knowledge.
- Equality is assessed across interactions, not minute by minute.
Source links:
- Woolley et al.: Evidence for a Collective Intelligence Factor
- Google: The Five Dynamics of Effective Teams
20. Q: Why can a team lose collective intelligence when one person dominates discussion?
A: Show answer
Persistent domination reduces the information available to the group. Other members may have relevant experience, notice a risk, or see a different interpretation but stay silent because they expect interruption or dismissal. The dominant speaker may then mistake silence for agreement. The team becomes vulnerable to blind spots because the same assumptions are repeatedly reinforced. Balanced participation does not remove leadership or expert input; it ensures that relevant knowledge can enter the discussion. Leaders can use structured invitations, pauses, written input, or round-robin contributions to prevent a few voices from controlling every stage of a decision.
Study instruction:
- "Identify a meeting with concentrated participation and propose two techniques to surface quieter members' knowledge."
Key point:
- Dominant participation can hide information and dissent.
- Structured opportunities to contribute improve access to group knowledge.
Source links:
- Woolley et al.: Evidence for a Collective Intelligence Factor
- Google: The Five Dynamics of Effective Teams
21. Q: What is social sensitivity in the context of team performance?
A: Show answer
Social sensitivity is the ability to notice and interpret cues such as tone of voice, facial expression, posture, hesitation, or signs of discomfort and exclusion. Socially sensitive teammates may notice when someone has not been heard or when a comment has created tension. They can check what is happening by asking a respectful question, rather than assuming they can read another person's mind. In the collective-intelligence research described in the chapter, average social sensitivity was associated with group performance. It helps members adjust their behavior and maintain participation, but cues are imperfect and should be verified through dialogue.
Study instruction:
- "Practice noticing a sign of disengagement and responding with a neutral question rather than an assumption."
Key point:
- Social sensitivity helps members notice how others are responding.
- Observed cues should prompt inquiry, not certainty about another person's thoughts.
Source links:
- Woolley et al.: Evidence for a Collective Intelligence Factor
22. Q: How does the “Reading the Mind in the Eyes” task relate to social sensitivity?
A: Show answer
The task asks participants to view photographs of people's eyes and choose which word among several alternatives best describes the emotion shown. It is one measure related to inferring another person's mental state from limited visual information. In the study described in the chapter, groups with higher average social sensitivity performed better across tasks. The test illustrates one aspect of attending to others' emotional cues, but it is not a complete measure of empathy, communication skill, or workplace suitability. It should not be used alone to hire, promote, or exclude people. Real social sensitivity includes listening, asking, checking interpretations, and responding appropriately in context.
Study instruction:
- "Describe what the task measures and explain why it should not be used as a complete measure of empathy or job suitability."
Key point:
- The task is one indicator related to interpreting emotional cues.
- A brief test cannot comprehensively assess a person's social or professional capability.
Source links:
- Woolley et al.: Evidence for a Collective Intelligence Factor
23. Q: What does the study's finding about gender composition mean for team selection?
A: Show answer
The study reported an association between the proportion of women in a group and its collective-intelligence score, alongside relationships involving social sensitivity and turn-taking. That is a research finding from a particular sample and design, not a universal causal rule or a direction to hire by sex. It does not establish that gender composition alone causes higher performance. The chapter's practical emphasis is on creating conditions in which members can participate and respond sensitively to one another. Organizations should follow fair, lawful selection practices and evaluate relevant competence, while ensuring that different people have meaningful opportunities to contribute. Correlations should not be converted into simplistic staffing formulas.
Study instruction:
- "Distinguish a statistical association from a causal conclusion or demographic hiring rule."
Key point:
- The reported gender relationship is an association, not a universal causal formula.
- Inclusive participation is an actionable practice; demographic shortcuts are not.
Source links:
- Woolley et al.: Evidence for a Collective Intelligence Factor
24. Q: Why does the chapter contrast the orderly Team A meeting with the informal Team B meeting?
A: Show answer
The comparison challenges the assumption that a polished meeting is necessarily more effective. Team A appears disciplined: members take turns, follow the agenda, and defer to an expert. Team B appears less orderly: members interrupt, follow tangents, and socialize. Additional information suggests that Team B members are more socially sensitive and participation is more balanced. The point is not that interruptions or irrelevant discussion are desirable. Rather, outward politeness and procedural order do not prove that the team is using its collective capability. Teams should be assessed by the quality of participation, understanding, problem-solving, and results, not by appearance alone.
Study instruction:
- "Explain why meeting neatness is an incomplete measure of collective effectiveness."
Key point:
- A polished meeting can still suppress relevant contributions.
- Assess whether members are heard and whether the team performs effectively.
Source links:
- Woolley et al.: Evidence for a Collective Intelligence Factor
25. Q: What five dynamics did Google identify as important to effective teams?
A: Show answer
Google's team-effectiveness framework identifies psychological safety, dependability, structure and clarity, meaning, and impact. Psychological safety concerns whether members can take interpersonal risks. Dependability concerns whether members complete quality work on time. Structure and clarity concern whether goals, roles, and plans are understood. Meaning concerns whether the work is personally important to members. Impact concerns whether members believe their work makes a meaningful difference. These conditions reinforce one another. A team can have a compelling purpose but still struggle if members cannot raise problems, or it can have a respectful climate but fail to deliver if responsibilities are unclear. The five dynamics should be reviewed as continuing team conditions, not one-time checklist items.
Study instruction:
- "Memorize all five dynamics and provide a specific workplace example for each."
Key point:
- The five dynamics are psychological safety, dependability, structure and clarity, meaning, and impact.
- Together they connect team climate, reliable work, coordination, and purpose.
Source links:
- Google: The Five Dynamics of Effective Teams
26. Q: What is the difference between meaning and impact in Google's team-effectiveness framework?
A: Show answer
Meaning concerns the personal significance of work to an individual. A task may matter because it connects to a person's values, interests, growth, or sense of contribution. Impact concerns the difference the team's work makes for customers, beneficiaries, the organization, or society. A project may be meaningful to an employee because it develops a valued skill, while its impact concerns whether the completed service actually improves an outcome for users. Both dimensions matter, but they answer different questions: why the work matters personally and what difference it makes beyond the team. Leaders should communicate both without exaggerating the importance of routine tasks.
Study instruction:
- "For one project, write separate statements about its personal meaning and its intended external impact."
Key point:
- Meaning is personal significance; impact is the difference the work makes.
- Teams should communicate both individual relevance and intended outcomes.
Source links:
- Google: The Five Dynamics of Effective Teams
27. Q: What does dependability mean in an effective team?
A: Show answer
Dependability means members can rely on one another to complete agreed work to the required standard and within the expected timeframe. It includes delivering outputs, communicating delays, raising risks early, maintaining accurate records, and following through on commitments. Dependability does not mean never making a mistake. A dependable colleague tells the team when a commitment is at risk and seeks assistance before the problem creates avoidable damage. Teams should make responsibilities, deadlines, and quality expectations explicit, and confirm important handoffs. Without dependability, even a psychologically safe team may struggle because members cannot plan around one another's work.
Study instruction:
- "Review a team commitment and identify its owner, output, deadline, quality standard, and escalation trigger."
Key point:
- Dependability is demonstrated through reliable delivery and early communication.
- Clear commitments support coordination and accountability.
Source links:
- Google: The Five Dynamics of Effective Teams
28. Q: What is meant by structure and clarity in a team?
A: Show answer
Structure and clarity mean members understand the team's goals, roles, responsibilities, decision rights, work plans, and measures of success. A team may be motivated and psychologically safe but still perform poorly if nobody knows who owns a task or how work is dependent on another person's output. Clarity reduces duplicated effort, conflicting instructions, and responsibility gaps. Structure should be sufficient to coordinate action without becoming so rigid that the team cannot adapt. When circumstances change, leaders should revise the plan and communicate changes to ownership, deadlines, and dependencies.
Study instruction:
- "Map a team's objectives, roles, dependencies, decision rights, and milestones; identify ambiguous ownership."
Key point:
- Structure and clarity make work understandable and coordinated.
- Clear roles should be updated when the environment or priorities change.
Source links:
- Google: The Five Dynamics of Effective Teams
29. Q: Why must a team believe its work is important?
A: Show answer
A credible belief that work is important helps members connect daily tasks with a worthwhile objective. Without that connection, tasks may feel like disconnected demands and it can be harder to prioritize or sustain effort. Leaders can explain how the team's work supports service quality, customer needs, organizational goals, or the people affected by its output. Importance should be described honestly rather than exaggerated. A clear purpose helps members understand trade-offs and why quality or timeliness matters. It also helps the team distinguish essential work from activity that consumes time without advancing its objectives.
Study instruction:
- "Write a plain-language explanation of why your team's work matters and identify the people affected by its success or failure."
Key point:
- Importance connects daily activities with a worthwhile objective.
- A credible purpose statement is specific, relevant, and honest.
Source links:
- Google: The Five Dynamics of Effective Teams
30. Q: How can psychological safety support the other four team dynamics?
A: Show answer
Psychological safety makes it easier for members to admit that a role is unclear, disclose a deadline risk, ask for help, or question whether the work is creating the intended impact. Without safety, people may remain silent about weaknesses in dependability, structure, meaning, or impact. Open communication allows the team to identify and improve those conditions. Safety does not create them automatically: leaders still need clear objectives, reliable work practices, meaningful tasks, and evidence of impact. It is an enabling condition that helps members discuss whether those other dynamics are present and what needs to change.
Study instruction:
- "For each of the five dynamics, identify one concern a member might hesitate to raise in an unsafe team."
Key point:
- Psychological safety helps teams discuss weaknesses in coordination and purpose.
- It supports improvement but does not replace clear goals or reliable delivery.
Source links:
- Google: The Five Dynamics of Effective Teams
- Harvard: Psychological Safety and Learning Behavior in Work Teams
31. Q: What role should a team leader play in building psychological safety?
A: Show answer
A leader should model the behaviors expected from everyone else. This includes listening without unnecessary interruption, acknowledging uncertainty, admitting mistakes, inviting questions, responding constructively to disagreement, and showing interest in members' concerns. Leaders also shape meeting routines and the consequences of speaking up. If a manager says feedback is welcome but reacts defensively to criticism, the actual norm is defined by the reaction, not the slogan. Psychological safety develops through repeated, observable interactions. Leaders should therefore make contributions visible, treat concerns fairly, and follow through when problems are raised.
Study instruction:
- "Review how a leader responds to errors, questions, and dissent; compare behavior with stated team values."
Key point:
- Leaders establish norms through consistent behavior rather than slogans.
- Listening, fallibility, and fair responses make candor more credible.
Source links:
- Harvard: Psychological Safety and Learning Behavior in Work Teams
- Google: The Five Dynamics of Effective Teams
32. Q: How can a leader give quieter members a meaningful opportunity to contribute?
A: Show answer
A leader can monitor participation and use meeting practices that create several ways to contribute. These include inviting each member's initial view, asking a person who has not spoken whether they see a risk, collecting written input before a meeting, using smaller discussions, and allowing reflection time before asking for a response. Invitations should be genuine rather than punitive. Leaders should also consider whether seniority, language, meeting format, or remote technology makes participation harder. The objective is not identical speaking time in every discussion; it is a fair opportunity for relevant knowledge and concerns to enter the team's work.
Study instruction:
- "Design a meeting routine that gives all members a realistic opportunity to contribute before a significant decision."
Key point:
- Participation requires deliberate facilitation when confidence or status differs.
- Written and small-group methods can complement open discussion.
Source links:
- Woolley et al.: Evidence for a Collective Intelligence Factor

33. Q: Why should a leader summarize what a team member has said?
A: Show answer
Summarizing shows that a contribution has been heard and lets the group correct misunderstandings. A leader can restate the main point, identify the evidence or concern behind it, and ask whether the summary is accurate. This helps members see that their input has entered the discussion and can reduce repetitive debate by establishing what has already been raised. A summary should not distort the speaker's position or imply agreement where none exists. The leader should preserve the contributor's meaning, invite correction, and distinguish a recorded concern from a final decision.
Study instruction:
- "Practice a neutral summary of a colleague's point and ask the colleague to confirm or correct your interpretation."
Key point:
- Reflective listening improves shared understanding and signals that contributions matter.
- A summary must not imply false agreement or change the speaker's meaning.
Source links:
- Google: The Five Dynamics of Effective Teams
- Harvard: Psychological Safety and Learning Behavior in Work Teams
34. Q: Why should leaders acknowledge uncertainty and admit their own mistakes?
A: Show answer
Acknowledging uncertainty shows that learning is legitimate and that authority does not require pretending to be infallible. When a leader says what is not yet known, members may feel more able to contribute overlooked facts or challenge an assumption. When a leader acknowledges an error and explains how it will be corrected, disclosure becomes more credible. This behavior should be genuine and proportionate; it should not become a performance that seeks reassurance or avoids responsibility. The aim is to normalize accurate communication about uncertainty while maintaining ownership of decisions and corrective actions.
Study instruction:
- "Prepare an example of a leader acknowledging uncertainty and inviting the team to verify the facts."
Key point:
- Visible fallibility can make it easier for others to speak honestly.
- Openness must be combined with responsibility and follow-through.
Source links:
- Harvard: Psychological Safety and Learning Behavior in Work Teams
- Google: The Five Dynamics of Effective Teams
35. Q: How should a team respond when a member reports a mistake or raises a concern?
A: Show answer
The team should listen without ridicule or premature blame, clarify what happened, and determine whether an immediate risk needs containment. Relevant facts should be recorded, including timing, affected work, decisions, and available evidence. Members should distinguish an honest error, a process weakness, a capability gap, and possible misconduct. The response should be proportionate and comply with applicable safety, compliance, or investigation procedures. The team should then agree on corrective action, ownership, deadlines, and how it will check whether the issue has been addressed. Respectful initial treatment encourages accurate disclosure; fair investigation maintains accountability.
Study instruction:
- "Rehearse a response that starts with fact-finding, addresses immediate risk, and ends with assigned corrective action."
Key point:
- Concerns require careful listening, factual investigation, and proportionate response.
- Respectful reporting and accountability should operate together.
Source links:
- Edmondson: Learning from Mistakes Is Easier Said Than Done
- Harvard: Psychological Safety and Learning Behavior in Work Teams
36. Q: How can a team avoid confusing loyalty with silence?
A: Show answer
A team should define loyalty as protecting the legitimate purpose and integrity of the work, not concealing information that could help it improve. Members can support one another while still reporting mistakes, questioning unsafe decisions, and challenging ineffective routines. When loyalty is interpreted as never criticizing a colleague or raising a concern outside the group, it creates pressure to withhold information. Leaders should explain that respectful escalation is sometimes necessary and that serious concerns must use appropriate channels. Genuine commitment includes responsibility to colleagues, users, beneficiaries, and the organization affected by team decisions.
Study instruction:
- "Identify a case where silence might be mistaken for loyalty and plan an appropriate way to raise the concern."
Key point:
- Commitment includes protecting the quality and integrity of the work.
- Loyalty must not suppress legitimate concerns or mandatory reporting.
Source links:
- Edmondson: Learning from Mistakes Is Easier Said Than Done
- Harvard: Psychological Safety and Learning Behavior in Work Teams
37. Q: What can teams learn from the contrast between enthusiasm norms and loyalty norms?
A: Show answer
Enthusiasm norms encourage members to explore suggestions, build on one another's ideas, and show interest in contributions. Loyalty norms can encourage solidarity, but they become restrictive when members feel they should not expose problems or question a group decision. Even positive-sounding values can have unintended consequences when interpreted rigidly. Encouraging enthusiasm does not mean accepting every proposal, and loyalty does not have to mean suppressing dissent. Teams should create room for curiosity, constructive criticism, and independent judgment while preserving mutual support. The practical test is whether a norm helps the group learn and deliver its objectives.
Study instruction:
- "Identify a team value that may have an unintended effect and rewrite it as an observable behavioral expectation."
Key point:
- The effect of a norm depends on how it changes actual behavior.
- Support and challenge should reinforce one another rather than compete.
Source links:
- Harvard: Psychological Safety and Learning Behavior in Work Teams
- Google: The Five Dynamics of Effective Teams
38. Q: How can a team preserve individual voices without letting individual egos undermine collaboration?
A: Show answer
A team should let members contribute distinct expertise, styles, and ideas while setting shared expectations for listening, respectful critique, and coordinated delivery. Preserving individual voices means people do not have to adopt one personality or suppress their perspective to belong. It does not mean members can ignore deadlines, dominate meetings, or reject decisions without process. Leaders can recognize distinctive contributions, distribute opportunities, and establish common standards for challenge and follow-through. The SNL example illustrates the balance: creative differences and competition remained, but members were expected to keep contributing and working together.
Study instruction:
- "Describe two ways to recognize individual contribution and two ways to maintain shared collaboration standards."
Key point:
- Individual expression and collective responsibility are compatible.
- Strong teams protect distinct contributions while maintaining common expectations.

- Google: The Five Dynamics of Effective Teams

39. Q: Why is social sensitivity important when a team experiences disagreement or tension?
A: Show answer
Disagreement can become destructive when members fail to notice how their words and behavior affect others. Socially sensitive teammates pay attention to signs that someone is confused, upset, excluded, or reluctant to continue. They can ask a clarifying question, slow the discussion, acknowledge a concern, or invite the person to explain. This does not require avoiding difficult topics; it helps keep the discussion focused on the issue without silencing participants. Members should verify impressions rather than assume that a facial expression or tone reveals someone's exact thoughts.
Study instruction:
- "Practice responding to a sign of disengagement with a neutral invitation to identify an unaddressed concern."
Key point:
- Social sensitivity helps members notice and respond to interpersonal signals.
- Checking an impression is safer than assuming what another person thinks.
Source links:
- Woolley et al.: Evidence for a Collective Intelligence Factor
- Harvard: Psychological Safety and Learning Behavior in Work Teams
40. Q: How should leaders balance meeting efficiency with inclusive participation?
A: Show answer
Leaders should distinguish unnecessary delay from the time needed to hear relevant information. Cutting off every discussion may make a meeting look efficient while suppressing risks and alternatives; allowing unrestricted debate can also waste time and obscure decisions. A balanced process sets a clear purpose, provides structured opportunities to contribute, manages interruptions, and records unresolved issues for follow-up. Time limits, round-robin input, written suggestions, and clear decision rules can make participation manageable. The standard should be whether the process supports a well-informed decision, not whether every discussion is perfectly smooth.
Study instruction:
- "Create an agenda for an important decision with an objective, structured input, discussion time, decision method, and action owners."
Key point:
- Efficiency should not be achieved by silencing relevant contributions.
- Meeting structure can protect participation while keeping discussion purposeful.
Source links:
- Woolley et al.: Evidence for a Collective Intelligence Factor
- Google: The Five Dynamics of Effective Teams
41. Q: What should a leader do when one team member repeatedly interrupts or dominates discussion?
A: Show answer
The leader should intervene neutrally and consistently to protect the discussion without humiliating the person. The leader can remind the group of the agreed norm, pause the speaker, invite the interrupted person to finish, or introduce structured turns. A private conversation may be appropriate when the behavior repeats. The manager should consider why domination occurs, such as seniority, expertise, anxiety, or a culture that rewards speed. The objective is not to silence confident or expert contributors; it is to prevent their participation from displacing the knowledge and perspectives of others.
Study instruction:
- "Write a respectful in-meeting intervention and a separate private feedback statement for repeated interruptions."
Key point:
- Leaders must protect other members' opportunity to contribute.
- Correct the behavior without turning the intervention into a personal attack.
Source links:
- Woolley et al.: Evidence for a Collective Intelligence Factor
- Google: The Five Dynamics of Effective Teams
42. Q: How can a team address exclusion or a member who feels unheard?
A: Show answer
The team should first understand what happened from the member's perspective and examine whether meeting practices, informal networks, status differences, or decision routines have excluded the person. The leader can ask what contribution the member wanted to make and what response they experienced. The team might then distribute agenda items, collect written input, rotate facilitation, or ensure relevant expertise is represented before deciding. General assurances that everyone is welcome are not enough. Follow-up is needed to determine whether the changed practices result in meaningful participation and influence.
Study instruction:
- "Prepare a response plan covering inquiry, process adjustment, and a review of whether participation improves."
Key point:
- Inclusion requires real opportunities to contribute and influence work.
- Recurring participation gaps should be investigated rather than dismissed.
Source links:
- Harvard: Psychological Safety and Learning Behavior in Work Teams
- Woolley et al.: Evidence for a Collective Intelligence Factor
43. Q: Does psychological safety require every member to vote on every decision?
A: Show answer
No. Psychological safety concerns the ability to contribute, ask questions, and raise concerns without unreasonable interpersonal punishment. It does not require consensus or identical decision authority. A team may assign final authority to a manager, technical specialist, or formal approval body while ensuring that relevant risks and information can be raised. Members should understand how decisions are made, when consultation is expected, and who has final responsibility. Having a voice is different from having a vote. Once a decision is reached, the team should understand its rationale and the responsibilities for implementation.
Study instruction:
- "Map a decision process and distinguish consultation, advisory input, approval authority, and implementation ownership."
Key point:
- Having a voice does not mean controlling every decision.
- Clear decision rights can coexist with open input and challenge.
Source links:
- Google: The Five Dynamics of Effective Teams
44. Q: How can psychological safety improve detection and correction of operational risks?
A: Show answer
People closest to a process often notice early warning signs before they appear in formal reports. They may detect a near miss, unclear instruction, workload problem, unusual transaction, or control weakness. If they fear ridicule or retaliation, they may keep the information to themselves. A psychologically safe team makes it easier to raise the concern while there is still time to investigate and contain it. The organization must connect speaking up to a functioning response system: record the report, assess the risk, assign an owner, implement corrective action, and monitor recurrence. Safety without action will lose credibility.
Study instruction:
- "Trace a risk report from first observation through escalation, investigation, correction, and monitoring."
Key point:
- Early warnings are useful only when people can raise them and the organization responds.
- Psychological safety should connect to documented risk and corrective-action systems.

- Edmondson: Learning from Mistakes Is Easier Said Than Done
- Harvard: Psychological Safety and Learning Behavior in Work Teams
45. Q: How does psychological safety support learning behavior?
A: Show answer
Learning behavior includes asking questions, seeking feedback, discussing errors, sharing information, experimenting, and reflecting on results. These actions can expose a person to embarrassment if the team treats uncertainty as incompetence. Psychological safety reduces that interpersonal barrier, making it more likely that members will surface information needed for collective learning. Edmondson's field study of work teams found a relationship between psychological safety and learning behavior and linked learning behavior with team performance in the studied setting. This does not mean safety alone guarantees performance: expertise, resources, task design, and leadership also matter.
Study instruction:
- "Identify examples of learning behavior and the interpersonal risk that might discourage each action."
Key point:
- Psychological safety encourages questions, feedback, and error discussion.
- Learning behavior is one pathway linking team climate with performance.
Source links:
- Harvard: Psychological Safety and Learning Behavior in Work Teams
46. Q: How should managers interpret an increase in reported errors after improving psychological safety?
A: Show answer
Managers should not automatically conclude that operations have deteriorated. More reports may mean employees are more willing to disclose incidents that were previously hidden. Management should examine event severity, actual incidence where measurable, near misses, reporting timeliness, repeated causes, and corrective-action completion. Independent quality checks, audits, and process reviews can provide additional evidence. Leaders should avoid targets that reward low reporting because they may encourage concealment. At the same time, any increase in actual error incidence must be investigated. The central task is to distinguish improved visibility from worsening performance.
Study instruction:
- "Design an incident dashboard that separates reported events, severity, repeated causes, reporting delay, and corrective-action status."
Key point:
- More reports can indicate greater transparency rather than more failures.
- Incident indicators need context and independent evidence.

- Edmondson: Learning from Mistakes Is Easier Said Than Done

47. Q: How can a team measure psychological safety without relying only on a satisfaction survey?
A: Show answer
A team can combine anonymous perception questions with observable behavior and follow-up evidence. Questions can ask whether members can admit mistakes, request help, disagree with a senior colleague, and receive a respectful response. Behavioral indicators may include the distribution of speaking time, concerns raised, near misses reported, questions asked, and quality of after-action reviews. Leaders should also examine whether reports lead to action and whether members across roles experience the climate similarly. No single metric proves that psychological safety exists. A credible assessment combines multiple sources, protects confidentiality, and uses findings to improve practice.
Study instruction:
- "Prepare five survey questions and three observable indicators for a team climate review."
Key point:
- Measure perceived safety and the behaviors it should make possible.
- Use several indicators and treat a single score as evidence, not proof.

48. Q: What should a leader do when their own behavior has damaged psychological safety?
A: Show answer
The leader should acknowledge the specific behavior and its effect, listen to affected members, and avoid defending the conduct before understanding what happened. Repair requires more than a general apology: the leader should identify what will change, invite feedback on whether the change is working, and demonstrate consistent follow-through. The team may need facilitated discussion, clearer meeting rules, or formal intervention if the conduct involved harassment, discrimination, retaliation, or other serious misconduct. Psychological safety is rebuilt through repeated evidence that speaking up receives a fair response. One positive meeting cannot erase a long pattern of punitive behavior.
Study instruction:
- "Create a repair plan that acknowledges behavior, hears its impact, defines changed conduct, and checks progress."
Key point:
- Repair requires accountability and sustained behavioral change.
- Serious misconduct may require formal procedures beyond informal discussion.

49. Q: How can an organization apply the chapter's lessons when forming a new team?
A: Show answer
The organization should clarify purpose, goals, roles, decision rights, and dependencies while establishing behavioral norms for participation, listening, disagreement, and error reporting. At the outset, members can agree how to raise concerns, handle interruptions, request help, resolve conflict, and document decisions. The leader should model these behaviors and periodically ask whether members can contribute. The team should track delivery and learning indicators, including whether commitments are met and problems are surfaced early. These practices must be adapted to the team's work, size, risks, and governance requirements. A kickoff workshop is useful only if agreements are applied consistently.
Study instruction:

Key point:
- Effective team design combines clarity of work with safe interaction norms.
- Team agreements must be demonstrated and reviewed in daily practice.

50. Q: How can a manager distinguish psychological safety from a lack of accountability?
A: Show answer
Psychological safety protects people from unnecessary interpersonal punishment for raising work-related issues; accountability clarifies what standards and responsibilities apply. They reinforce each other when members can report problems early and then take part in a fair process to correct them. A safe team does not ignore missed commitments, unsafe conduct, poor-quality work, or dishonesty. It examines facts, identifies responsibility, provides feedback, and monitors improvement without humiliating people or discouraging disclosure. A manager should be clear about performance expectations while ensuring that questions and honest error reports are treated as useful information rather than evidence of disloyalty.
Study instruction:
- "Develop an example that combines candid reporting, clear standards, fair investigation, and follow-through."
Key point:
- Psychological safety and accountability are complementary, not opposites.
- Fair standards require both honest disclosure and proportionate consequences.

51. Q: What warning signs suggest that a team lacks psychological safety?
A: Show answer
Warning signs include repeated silence after leaders ask for input, members avoiding questions, mistakes being concealed until discovered, frequent private complaints, reluctance to request help, and decisions that receive no visible challenge despite significant uncertainty. Other signs include interruption patterns that silence some roles, defensive reactions to feedback, blame before fact-finding, and members agreeing publicly while expressing different views privately. No single behavior proves that a team is unsafe; context matters. Leaders should examine patterns across meetings, incidents, roles, and time, then ask members what prevents them from speaking openly. The response should address causes rather than simply instructing people to be more confident.
Study instruction:
- "Identify three warning signs in a team and gather evidence to test whether they reflect a recurring pattern."
Key point:
- Silence, concealment, and defensive reactions can signal an unsafe climate.
- Assess repeated patterns and causes rather than relying on one incident.

52. Q: How can a team charter translate psychological safety into practical expectations?
A: Show answer
A useful charter states the team's purpose, expected outcomes, roles, decision rights, meeting practices, and procedures for raising concerns. Behavioral commitments might include listening without unnecessary interruption, inviting dissent, documenting decisions, acknowledging uncertainty, reporting errors promptly, and escalating safety or compliance issues through formal channels. The charter should explain how disagreements will be resolved and how commitments will be monitored. It should be short enough to use and specific enough to guide behavior. Leaders should review it after major changes or incidents and ask members whether the agreed norms are visible in daily work.
Study instruction:

53. Q: How can psychological safety be maintained in remote or hybrid teams?
A: Show answer
Remote and hybrid teams need deliberate ways to make participation and concerns visible because digital channels can obscure tone, hesitation, and nonverbal cues. Leaders should agree which channels are suitable for routine updates, sensitive feedback, and urgent escalation. They can distribute agendas in advance, invite written input, ensure remote participants can contribute without competing with an in-room group, and summarize decisions with owners and deadlines. Video meetings should not be treated as the only route to participation; asynchronous channels may help people who need reflection time. The team should also check that messages are acknowledged and that digital records do not substitute for a needed conversation.

54. Q: What should a manager do when a team member repeatedly fails to meet commitments?
A: Show answer
The manager should clarify the commitment, identify the specific missed deliverable or deadline, and discuss its operational impact. The conversation should establish whether the cause was unclear ownership, competing priorities, insufficient resources, capability, or failure to follow through. The parties should agree on a realistic plan, responsible owner, deadline, quality expectation, and early-warning process if delivery becomes at risk. Repeated failures should be documented and addressed through normal performance or escalation procedures. Psychological safety means the employee can explain constraints honestly; it does not mean that the commitment can be ignored or accountability removed.
Study instruction:

- Dependability requires clear commitments and early communication about risk.

55. Q: How can teams manage disagreement between members with different expertise or professional backgrounds?
A: Show answer
The team should make the underlying assumptions and evidence explicit, clarify which expertise is relevant to the decision, and invite each specialist to explain risks in language the whole group can understand. Members should avoid treating professional status as a substitute for evidence or assuming that one discipline sees every consequence. A structured discussion can compare options, surface dependencies, and identify unresolved questions. The decision-maker should record the rationale and any dissent that remains material. Psychological safety helps participants question one another without personal attack, while structure and clarity prevent debate from becoming an indefinite contest of authority.
Study instruction:
- "Use a cross-functional decision and identify the evidence each discipline should contribute."
Key point:
- Different expertise can improve decisions when members can explain and challenge assumptions.
- Authority and expertise should be relevant to the issue, not used to silence other evidence.

56. Q: Why is psychological safety particularly important when work is complex or uncertain?
A: Show answer
Complex work contains dependencies, incomplete information, and changing conditions that no individual can fully monitor. Members may notice different parts of the problem, so the team needs questions, warnings, and requests for help to move across roles. If people hide uncertainty or avoid challenging assumptions, the group may act on an incomplete picture. Psychological safety supports early disclosure and collective adjustment. It does not remove uncertainty or guarantee the correct answer; it makes it more likely that the team will identify what it does not know, test assumptions, and update decisions as evidence changes.
Study instruction:
- "Select a complex project and list information that only different team roles may possess."
Key point:
- Complex work needs distributed information and frequent clarification.
- Safety helps uncertainty and emerging problems become visible earlier.

57. Q: How can a team avoid groupthink while preserving cooperation?
A: Show answer
The team should distinguish cooperation from pressure to agree. Leaders can ask members to identify assumptions, consider alternative explanations, assign a reviewer to test a proposal, and invite a dissenting view before a decision is finalized. Members should be allowed to challenge the plan without being treated as disloyal. The team can then use agreed decision rules to resolve the issue and move forward. Groupthink is not prevented by permanent argument; it is reduced when relevant alternatives and risks are genuinely considered. Psychological safety supports dissent, while clear roles and deadlines prevent discussion from becoming endless.
Study instruction:
- "Design a decision review that includes alternative options, a challenge role, evidence checks, and a clear decision point."
Key point:
- Cooperation should not require automatic agreement.
- Structured dissent can improve analysis without preventing timely decisions.

58. Q: How can leaders use meeting routines to reinforce social sensitivity?
A: Show answer
Leaders can establish simple routines that encourage attention to others: allow speakers to finish, summarize contributions, notice who has not been heard, check how a decision affects different roles, and pause when tension rises. They can ask whether anyone sees an overlooked consequence and invite members to clarify concerns rather than guess at their feelings. Routines work best when leaders use them consistently and explain their purpose. Social sensitivity should not become intrusive monitoring or an excuse to infer private emotions. It is a practical discipline of noticing, asking, listening, and adjusting behavior so that the group can work effectively.
Study instruction:
- "Create a short meeting checklist that prompts listening, balanced participation, and checks for overlooked concerns."
Key point:
- Social sensitivity becomes useful through observable listening and inquiry.
- Leaders should verify cues rather than claim to know unspoken feelings.

59. Q: What is the relationship between psychological safety and team dependability?
A: Show answer
Psychological safety helps members disclose when a commitment is at risk, ask for assistance, and admit that a task may not be completed as planned. These behaviors improve dependability because problems are surfaced before they become missed deadlines or poor-quality handoffs. Dependability, in turn, reinforces safety when members see that colleagues communicate honestly and follow through. A team needs both: a supportive climate without reliable delivery will disappoint stakeholders, while strict delivery expectations without safe reporting can conceal delays and errors. Clear owners, deadlines, standards, and escalation triggers connect the two dynamics in day-to-day work.
Study instruction:

- Safety helps members report delivery risks before failure occurs.
- Dependability requires both reliable work and honest communication.

60. Q: How should a team respond when a member proposes an unconventional or apparently impractical idea?
A: Show answer
The team should clarify the problem the idea is intended to solve and explore its assumptions before dismissing it. Members can ask what evidence would support the idea, what constraints matter, and whether a small test could reveal its value. Not every proposal deserves equal resources, but early ridicule can stop useful ideas from emerging. The SNL example shows how allowing unusual suggestions to be explored can support creative work. A productive team maintains standards by evaluating the idea's merits while keeping respect for the person who raised it. A proposal can be rejected without making future contributions feel unsafe.

61. Q: How can an organization distinguish a safe reporting culture from a culture with genuinely worsening performance?
A: Show answer
The organization should combine employee reporting with independent indicators of actual outcomes. It can examine incident severity, repeat causes, audit findings, quality inspections, near misses, customer or beneficiary impact, and whether corrective actions are completed. A rise in reporting alongside more timely disclosures and fewer repeat incidents may indicate improved transparency. A rise in severe events, repeat failures, or unresolved corrective actions may signal deteriorating performance. The analysis should consider changes in workload, exposure, staffing, and reporting rules. Managers should never encourage low incident counts as a proxy for safety without checking whether the data reflects concealment.

62. Q: How can the five team dynamics be used to diagnose a struggling team?
A: Show answer
A diagnosis can review each dynamic separately. Ask whether members can take interpersonal risks; whether commitments are completed reliably; whether goals, roles, and plans are clear; whether members find the work personally meaningful; and whether they can see the work's impact. Evidence may come from interviews, meeting observation, delivery records, staff feedback, and project outcomes. Avoid assuming that one low score explains every problem. For example, repeated delays may reflect unclear ownership rather than low motivation, while silence about risks may reflect low psychological safety. The team should identify root causes and choose specific changes that can be reviewed over time.
Study instruction:

- The five dynamics provide a structured diagnostic lens.

63. Q: What is the difference between having a voice and having decision authority?
A: Show answer
Having a voice means a member can contribute information, question assumptions, and raise relevant concerns. Decision authority means having formal responsibility to choose or approve an action. These rights are not identical. A team member may have valuable operational knowledge without owning the final decision, while a designated manager may have approval authority but lack some frontline information. Effective teams make both roles clear: who must be consulted, who decides, who approves, and who implements. Psychological safety supports the flow of information even when final authority is concentrated. Clear authority prevents open participation from being confused with a requirement for consensus.
Study instruction:
- "Map one decision into consultation, recommendation, approval, and implementation roles."
Key point:
- Voice enables information to enter a decision; authority determines who decides.
- Clear rights prevent confusion without suppressing relevant input.

A: Show answer
In high-accountability settings, speaking up can be essential to patient, staff, beneficiary, financial, or operational safety. Teams should make it possible to question instructions, report near misses, raise safeguarding concerns, and disclose control weaknesses while following formal escalation and documentation requirements. Leaders should respond without retaliation, protect confidentiality where required, and ensure that concerns reach people with authority to act. Psychological safety must not be used to replace incident protocols, professional standards, donor conditions, or regulatory duties. The chapter's practical lesson is to pair an open climate with clear responsibilities, evidence, and timely corrective action.

65. Q: What should be included in a follow-up review after a team has agreed to improve its working norms?
A: Show answer
A follow-up review should determine whether the agreed behaviors have changed and whether the change is improving team work. It can examine meeting participation, response to questions, error reporting, completion of commitments, clarity of roles, and whether members can raise concerns without retaliation. The review should collect feedback from different roles and compare it with observable evidence. Leaders should identify practices that are working, barriers that remain, and any new actions with named owners and dates. The team should avoid treating a one-off workshop or a positive survey as proof that its culture has changed. Norms are sustained through repeated practice.
