Table 3
Focus, Cognitive Tunneling, and Mental Models
1. Q: What is the central argument of Chapter 3, “Focus: Cognitive Tunneling, Air France Flight 447, and the Power of Mental Models”?
A: Show answer
The chapter argues that effective focus is not simply a matter of trying harder to concentrate. People must actively decide what deserves attention, anticipate what they are likely to encounter, and maintain a useful mental picture of how a situation works. Automation and habitual responses can reduce routine workload, but they can also leave people unprepared when an unexpected event suddenly requires judgment. Under pressure, attention may narrow around the most obvious cue, while important information is overlooked. Mental models help prevent this by giving the mind an organized expectation against which new information can be compared. The chapter contrasts the Air France Flight 447 accident, where the crew struggled to interpret a rapidly changing situation, with the Qantas Flight 32 emergency, where the crew had rehearsed scenarios, challenged assumptions, and revised its model as evidence changed.
Study instruction:
- Explain how automation, cognitive tunneling, reactive thinking, and mental models are connected, using both flight examples to illustrate the differences.
Key point:
- Focus improves when attention is deliberately directed and supported by an updated mental model.
- Automation is useful, but it does not remove the need for human judgment and monitoring.
Source links:
- BEA: Air France Flight AF447 investigation
- ATSB: Qantas Flight QF32 investigation
- NASA: Flight Cognition Laboratory
2. Q: What does the chapter mean by describing attention as a spotlight?
A: Show answer
The spotlight is a metaphor for the way people select some information for closer processing while allowing other information to remain in the background. A person may deliberately widen attention to understand a whole situation or narrow it to inspect one detail. Attention is influenced by intentions, expectations, workload, emotional state, and the salience of incoming information. The metaphor does not mean that the brain has a literal beam or that people can consciously control every attentional process. It illustrates that attention is selective and that the selected focus can be helpful or harmful. A pilot, nurse, manager, or driver may concentrate intensely on one display, patient, message, or warning while failing to notice a more important change elsewhere. Effective focus therefore requires checking whether the current object of attention still deserves priority.
Study instruction:
- Identify a recent situation in which you focused on one detail and list other information that may have deserved attention.
Key point:
- Attention is selective rather than unlimited or evenly distributed.
- The most noticeable cue is not necessarily the most important cue.
Source links:
- NASA: Flight Cognition Laboratory
3. Q: Why should attention be treated as a limited resource?
A: Show answer
People have finite capacity to process competing information, monitor several activities, and maintain awareness of changing conditions. When many tasks demand attention at once, some information receives less scrutiny, task steps may be forgotten, and switching between activities can create gaps. The Federal Aviation Administration's ACT ARC Recommendation 20-1 explicitly treats attention as a limited resource and recommends deliberate allocation of attention, workload planning, and recognition of attentional capture. The practical implication is not that people must concentrate continuously on everything. They should rank tasks by consequence, schedule demanding work when capacity is available, protect time for critical monitoring, and avoid adding unnecessary interruptions during high-risk periods. In ordinary office work, this may mean closing unrelated applications while reviewing a high-value payment or postponing nonurgent messages during a complex analysis.
Study instruction:
- Map a demanding work period, rank its tasks by consequence, and identify where attention is likely to be overloaded or divided.
Key point:
- Attention must be allocated according to task priority and risk.
- Reducing avoidable competing demands improves the chance of noticing important information.
Source links:
- FAA ACT ARC Recommendation 20-1: Managing Attention and Workload
- NASA: Flight Cognition Laboratory
4. Q: What is cognitive tunneling, and how does it affect decision-making?
A: Show answer
Cognitive tunneling is an attentional failure in which a person becomes excessively focused on a narrow cue, task, or display and fails to process other information needed to understand the situation. The chapter describes it as especially dangerous when attention has been relaxed during routine automation and must suddenly become highly focused during an unexpected event. The mind may seize on the most obvious stimulus because it is immediately available, even when that stimulus is not the best guide to action. A person may repeatedly inspect an error message while overlooking a worsening system condition, or keep correcting one visible discrepancy while ignoring evidence that the underlying problem is different. Cognitive tunneling is not simply ordinary concentration; it is an unhelpful narrowing that prevents the person from integrating the wider situation. Recovery requires deliberately broadening the scan, checking critical indicators, and comparing observations with an explicit model of what should be happening.
Study instruction:
- Describe a realistic work or safety scenario in which attention could become trapped on one cue, then specify how the person could broaden the scan.
Key point:
- Cognitive tunneling makes one cue dominate attention at the expense of the wider situation.
- Deliberate scanning and model checking help restore situational awareness.
Source links:
- NASA: Flight Cognition Laboratory
- FAA ACT ARC Recommendation 20-1
5. Q: Why can the transition from automated operation to manual intervention create an attention problem?
A: Show answer
During stable automated operation, people may shift from active control to supervisory monitoring. This can reduce routine workload, but it may also reduce the frequency with which operators actively interpret the system, practice manual skills, or maintain a detailed picture of its current state. If an unexpected event then removes or changes the automation, the operator must rapidly reconstruct the situation while alarms, messages, and time pressure compete for attention. The transition itself can therefore be demanding: the operator must recognize that the operating mode has changed, understand what the system is now doing, identify which information is trustworthy, and choose an appropriate response. This is why automation training should include abnormal situations, mode awareness, and deliberate monitoring rather than only normal operation. The appropriate response to a real system failure must always follow the relevant approved procedures and training, not a generic instruction to override automation.
Study instruction:
- Choose an automated process you use and identify what would change if the automation stopped unexpectedly, including the information and skills needed to take over safely.
Key point:
- Automation can reduce routine activity while increasing the importance of readiness for abnormal conditions.
- Operators need an accurate understanding of system modes and fallback procedures.
Source links:
- FAA AC 120-71B: Standard Operating Procedures and Pilot Monitoring Duties
- NASA: Flight Cognition Laboratory
6. Q: What sequence of events in Air France Flight 447 illustrates the danger of losing situational awareness?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes Flight 447 as an example of a complex emergency in which an initial sensor problem was followed by a breakdown in interpretation and control. Ice crystals affected the aircraft's pitot probes, producing unreliable airspeed indications and a change in the automated flight-control situation. The pilots then had to interpret warnings and manage the aircraft manually. The chapter emphasizes that the crew's attention became concentrated on particular displays and messages while the aircraft's changing flight path and aerodynamic condition were not correctly integrated. The official French investigation identifies pitot-probe icing, erroneous speed indications, a stall, and impact with the ocean as central elements of the accident. The chapter's concept of cognitive tunneling is an explanatory human-factors lens; it should not be treated as the sole official finding or as a substitute for the full investigation. The event demonstrates how technical disturbances, mode changes, workload, coordination, and human interpretation can interact.
Study instruction:
- Reconstruct the broad event sequence and distinguish the technical trigger, the changing flight condition, the crew's interpretation, and the final outcome.
Key point:
- A technical disturbance can become more dangerous when the crew cannot maintain a coherent picture of the aircraft's state.
- The BEA investigation, not a simplified anecdote, is the authority for the accident's official findings.
Source links:
- BEA: Air France Flight AF447 investigation and final report
7. Q: What are pitot probes, and why did their icing matter during Flight 447?
A: Show answer
Pitot probes are aircraft sensors that measure the pressure associated with airflow and provide information used to calculate airspeed. Reliable airspeed information is important because pilots and flight-control systems use it to assess the aircraft's energy state and whether it is operating within safe limits. During Flight 447, ice crystals obstructed the probes and the resulting airspeed indications became unreliable. The loss of trustworthy speed information affected the automated flight-control system and added uncertainty to the crew's task. The important general lesson is that a sensor value is not automatically a reliable description of reality. Operators must understand what a sensor measures, how it can fail, what other indicators can corroborate it, and which procedures apply when readings become inconsistent. In finance or operations, the equivalent is checking whether a dashboard figure is supported by the underlying transaction records before acting on it.
Study instruction:
- Select a sensor or key data field in your own work and document what it measures, how it could become unreliable, and what independent evidence could confirm it.
Key point:
- Decisions based on faulty inputs can be internally consistent but still lead to an incorrect conclusion.
- Critical measurements should be interpreted in context and checked against appropriate independent information.
Source links:
- BEA: Air France Flight AF447 investigation
8. Q: How did fixation on an instrument display contribute to the chapter's explanation of Flight 447?
A: Show answer
The chapter explains that one pilot became preoccupied with an aircraft symbol and its position on a primary flight display, while the other concentrated on computer-generated messages. Each pilot was attending to information that appeared salient and actionable, but neither maintained a sufficiently integrated view of the aircraft's actual flight path and condition. The danger was not that displays are inherently harmful; instruments are essential sources of information. The problem was allowing one display or message stream to dominate attention without cross-checking it against other critical indications and the aircraft's response. A similar failure can occur when an analyst repeatedly refreshes a dashboard, a technician focuses on one alarm, or a manager watches a single performance metric while ignoring contradictory operational evidence. Effective monitoring combines focused inspection with a wider scan and active verification.
Study instruction:
- Identify a dashboard or screen that can capture your attention and list the other indicators that must be checked before making a decision.
Key point:
- A useful display becomes dangerous when it replaces rather than supports broader situational awareness.
- Cross-check the information source, the system response, and the real-world outcome.
Source links:
- BEA: Air France Flight AF447 investigation
- FAA ACT ARC Recommendation 20-1
9. Q: What is the purpose of a pilot-monitoring role, and what can go wrong when it is not performed effectively?
A: Show answer
A pilot-monitoring role is intended to provide an independent layer of observation, communication, and cross-checking while the other pilot performs the primary flying tasks. Monitoring should not become passive reading of screens or completion of secondary tasks without regard to the aircraft's state. The monitoring pilot needs to maintain awareness of the flight path, verify relevant modes and changes, notice when the other pilot's actions do not match the agreed plan, and speak up clearly when necessary. The chapter's Flight 447 narrative illustrates how both pilots can become absorbed in separate cues and fail to provide effective mutual monitoring. The Federal Aviation Administration's guidance emphasizes the importance of defined pilot-monitoring duties and integrating them into standard operating procedures. In other workplaces, the equivalent is assigning a reviewer who independently checks the critical outcome rather than merely repeating the preparer's steps.
Study instruction:
- Review a two-person control process and define what the primary operator and independent reviewer must each observe, communicate, and verify.
Key point:
- Monitoring is an active responsibility, not simply watching a screen or waiting for an error.
- Clear roles and independent cross-checks reduce the risk of shared fixation.
Source links:
- FAA AC 120-71B: Pilot Monitoring Duties
- BEA: Air France Flight AF447 investigation
10. Q: What is reactive thinking, and why can it be both useful and dangerous?
A: Show answer
Reactive thinking is the use of learned responses that can be executed quickly with little deliberate analysis. Repeated practice allows people to perform familiar actions efficiently, such as following a routine, recognizing a common pattern, or completing a well-learned procedure. This is valuable when the situation is stable and the response remains appropriate. It becomes dangerous when a person applies a familiar action to a materially different situation. The chapter uses Flight 447 to illustrate how a practiced response associated with one kind of emergency may be unsuitable in another flight condition. The risk is not habit itself but failure to notice that the context has changed. A useful safeguard is to pause long enough to identify the current conditions, confirm that the learned response applies, and use approved procedures where safety is involved.
Study instruction:
- List three routines you perform automatically and identify the conditions that would make each routine inappropriate or require a different response.
Key point:
- Habits improve speed and consistency in familiar conditions.
- Context checking is essential before applying a practiced response to an unfamiliar event.
Source links:
- NASA: Flight Cognition Laboratory
- FAA ACT ARC Recommendation 20-1
11. Q: When does automaticity improve performance rather than undermine it?
A: Show answer
Automaticity is useful when a task is sufficiently familiar, the environment is stable, and the learned response has been validated for the conditions in which it is used. It reduces the mental effort required for repetitive activities and leaves more capacity for planning, monitoring, and problem-solving. Examples include using a standard checklist, recognizing a familiar spreadsheet error, or following a routine approval workflow. Automaticity becomes less reliable when the task changes, an unusual exception appears, or a familiar cue has a different meaning in the current context. People should therefore automate routine steps while retaining checkpoints that detect exceptions and confirm that the process is still operating as expected. A well-designed process makes normal work efficient without making abnormal conditions invisible.
Study instruction:
- Separate a recurring task into routine steps that can be standardized and exception points that require deliberate judgment.
Key point:
- Automaticity is most useful when conditions match the assumptions behind the learned response.
- Exception monitoring prevents routine efficiency from becoming blind repetition.
Source links:
- NASA: Flight Cognition Laboratory
12. Q: Why can a familiar response become a trap during an unfamiliar emergency?
A: Show answer
A familiar response can become a trap when a person retrieves a well-practiced action before adequately diagnosing the current situation. Familiarity creates speed, but it can also make a response feel correct simply because it has worked before. In an unfamiliar emergency, the underlying conditions may differ in important ways, so the old response may worsen the problem. The chapter's comparison of Flight 447 and Qantas Flight 32 shows why operators need to ask what is happening now rather than merely recall what they did in a previous exercise. The practical safeguard is to verify the problem, identify the constraints, check the relevant procedure, and monitor the effect of each action. In non-aviation settings, a manager should not reuse a previous crisis solution without checking whether the current causes, resources, authority, and risks are comparable.
Study instruction:
- Choose a past problem-solving success and specify the conditions that must be present before you reuse the same response.
Key point:
- Familiarity is not proof that a response fits the current situation.
- Diagnose the present conditions before transferring a past solution.
Source links:
- NASA: Flight Cognition Laboratory
- ATSB: Qantas Flight QF32 investigation
13. Q: How is cognitive tunneling different from ordinary distraction, confirmation bias, and automation bias?
A: Show answer
Ordinary distraction occurs when attention is diverted from the current task, often by an external interruption. Cognitive tunneling is a more specific narrowing in which attention becomes locked onto a limited cue or activity and other relevant information is not adequately processed. Confirmation bias is the tendency to seek, interpret, or favor evidence that supports an existing belief. Automation bias is the tendency to over-rely on an automated recommendation or output, even when other evidence conflicts with it. These mechanisms can overlap: a person may be distracted by an alert, tunnel onto the alert's text, accept the automated interpretation, and ignore evidence that contradicts an existing assumption. The distinctions matter because the countermeasures differ. Reducing interruptions helps with distraction; broad scanning helps with tunneling; actively seeking disconfirming evidence helps with confirmation bias; and independent verification helps with automation bias.
Study instruction:
- Analyze one decision failure and identify whether distraction, attentional narrowing, confirmation bias, automation bias, or a combination contributed to it.
Key point:
- These are related but distinct ways that information processing can fail.
- Match the corrective control to the specific failure mechanism.
Source links:
- NASA: Flight Cognition Laboratory
- FAA ACT ARC Recommendation 20-1
14. Q: What is a mental model, and how does it help a person manage attention?
A: Show answer
A mental model is an internal representation of how a situation, process, or system is expected to work. It may include the important elements, the relationships between them, the sequence of events, likely outcomes, and warning signs that would indicate a deviation. A nurse may carry an expectation of what a healthy infant looks like; a pilot may anticipate how an aircraft should respond to a control input; a manager may picture the dependencies in a project. A useful model helps the person notice discrepancies because incoming information can be compared with an expectation. It also helps determine which details deserve attention and which are routine. Mental models are not perfect copies of reality; they are working explanations that must be tested and revised as new information becomes available.
Study instruction:
- Draw a simple mental model of a process you know, showing its main components, expected sequence, and signals that would indicate a problem.
Key point:
- Mental models organize information and make unexpected deviations easier to notice.
- A model is useful only when it remains open to testing and revision.
Source links:
- NASA: Flight Cognition Laboratory
15. Q: How is a mental model different from a checklist, plan, or written procedure?
A: Show answer
A checklist or procedure specifies actions or checks that should be completed; a plan describes intended steps toward an objective; a mental model explains how the situation is expected to behave and why particular cues matter. These tools complement one another. A checklist can reduce omission risk, while a mental model helps the operator recognize when the checklist does not fit the actual condition or when the situation has changed. A plan can organize work, while a mental model helps anticipate dependencies and consequences. None should replace the others. In a high-risk environment, personnel should follow approved procedures and use their understanding of the system to recognize anomalies and seek the appropriate escalation path. In an office process, a checklist may confirm that an invoice was reviewed, while a mental model helps the reviewer notice that the transaction does not make sense in the context of the project or contract.
Study instruction:
- Select a procedure you use and identify which parts are covered by the checklist and which require an understanding of how the process works.
Key point:
- Checklists guide action; mental models support interpretation and adaptation.
- Strong performance uses both structured procedures and situational understanding.
Source links:
- NASA: Flight Cognition Laboratory
- FAA AC 120-71B: Standard Operating Procedures and Pilot Monitoring Duties
16. Q: What did Darlene's neonatal intensive-care experience illustrate about mental models?
A: Show answer
Darlene noticed a newborn whose skin appeared mottled, whose abdomen seemed distended, and whose heel dressing showed more blood than she expected. Each sign could have seemed minor when viewed alone, and the routine vital-sign readings did not immediately establish that the infant was deteriorating. Darlene combined the details with her mental picture of what a healthy baby in that setting should look like. The mismatch prompted her to examine the infant and alert the physician; subsequent tests indicated early sepsis. The chapter uses this story to show how an experienced professional can recognize a pattern before being able to explain every element consciously. It is an illustration of attention and expertise, not a clinical diagnostic rule. Medical assessment, monitoring protocols, and professional judgment remain essential.
Study instruction:
- Identify how several individually weak signals can become meaningful when interpreted together, and explain why the story must not be used as a substitute for clinical protocols.
Key point:
- A mental model can make subtle deviations visible when isolated data points appear unremarkable.
- Pattern recognition should prompt verification, not replace evidence or professional procedures.
Source links:
- NASA: Flight Cognition Laboratory
17. Q: How does expert intuition differ from an unexplained hunch?
A: Show answer
Expert intuition can reflect a large store of experience organized into patterns that the person recognizes quickly. A skilled professional may notice that several small cues do not fit the expected situation even before being able to describe exactly why. The chapter's account of Darlene illustrates this process: her apparent hunch became clearer when she explained the picture of a healthy infant that she carried in her mind. However, intuition is not automatically correct. It can be distorted by fatigue, incomplete experience, bias, or a misleading resemblance to a familiar case. A strong professional response is to treat intuition as a signal to investigate, identify the observations behind it, seek corroborating information, and follow the appropriate procedure. This combines the speed of pattern recognition with the discipline of verification.
Study instruction:
- Recall a time when you sensed that something was wrong and reconstruct the specific cues, assumptions, and evidence behind that judgment.
Key point:
- Expertise can make patterns recognizable before they are fully verbalized.
- A hunch should trigger structured checking rather than automatic acceptance.
Source links:
- NASA: Flight Cognition Laboratory
18. Q: How can expectations help attention, and how can they also create blind spots?
A: Show answer
Expectations help attention by providing a reference point for what is normal, likely, or important. When reality differs from that reference, the discrepancy can attract attention and prompt investigation. However, expectations can also create blind spots if a person becomes attached to one explanation and dismisses evidence that does not fit. A mental model that is too rigid may cause the operator to interpret new information as noise or error rather than as a reason to revise the model. For example, a project manager who expects a supplier to deliver on time may discount early warning signs of delay. The safeguard is to make predictions explicit, identify what evidence would disprove them, and update the model when observations conflict with expectations. A good model sharpens attention without predetermining the conclusion.
Study instruction:
- Write down one expectation about a current project and specify two observations that would require you to revise it.
Key point:
- Expectations make deviations visible, but rigid expectations can suppress contradictory evidence.
- Effective mental models include conditions for revision.
Source links:
- NASA: Flight Cognition Laboratory
19. Q: Why does the chapter recommend telling yourself stories about what is happening?
A: Show answer
Narrating events creates a running explanation of what has happened, what may happen next, and how current details fit together. This practice encourages people to connect observations rather than treat each message, action, or event as an isolated item. It can also make future situations more concrete: a person can imagine how a meeting may unfold, what questions may arise, or which signs would indicate that a process is going off course. The chapter describes this as a habit of forecasting and building mental models. Storytelling is not a license to invent motives or assume that a prediction is true. It is a way to make expectations explicit so that reality can be compared with them. The model should remain provisional and be challenged by other people and new evidence.
Study instruction:
- Before one important activity, write a short narrative of the expected sequence, likely questions, and possible deviations; compare it with what actually occurs.
Key point:
- Narration helps connect information and prepare attention for relevant changes.
- Predictions should be treated as hypotheses that can be corrected.
Source links:
- NASA: Flight Cognition Laboratory
20. Q: How can a person deliberately build a mental model before starting a task?
A: Show answer
Begin by clarifying the task's objective and the result that would count as success. Identify the main stages, dependencies, people, tools, constraints, and likely sources of error. Then imagine the sequence in enough detail to anticipate what information will be needed and what could change. For a meeting, this might include the decision to be made, the participants' likely concerns, the evidence required, and the questions that could challenge the proposal. For a reconciliation, it might include expected balances, source records, matching rules, and exceptions. The model should be simple enough to guide attention but detailed enough to reveal meaningful deviations. It should also include uncertainty: identify what is not yet known and what information would change the plan.
Study instruction:
- Prepare a one-page task model showing the objective, stages, dependencies, expected signals, risks, and unresolved assumptions.
Key point:
- Mental preparation is more useful when it anticipates both the expected sequence and possible deviations.
- A model should expose assumptions instead of hiding them.
Source links:
- NASA: Flight Cognition Laboratory
21. Q: How should a person test whether a mental model is accurate?
A: Show answer
A mental model should be tested against observable results, independent evidence, and alternative explanations. First, state what the model predicts. Next, identify what evidence would support or contradict that prediction. Compare the prediction with actual outcomes and ask whether a different explanation fits the evidence better. Where possible, test the model on a small scale before relying on it for a consequential decision. In a project, a manager might predict that a staffing change will reduce delays; the model should specify which delays should improve and how the result will be measured. If the expected improvement does not occur, the manager should examine the assumptions rather than simply repeat the same intervention. A model that cannot be challenged is a belief, not a reliable decision aid.
Study instruction:
- Convert one assumption into a testable prediction and define the evidence and review date that will determine whether the model needs revision.
Key point:
- A useful model makes predictions that can be checked against reality.
- Contradictory evidence should lead to investigation and revision, not automatic dismissal.
Source links:
- NASA: Flight Cognition Laboratory
22. Q: Why is it useful to maintain more than one possible mental model during an uncertain situation?
A: Show answer
Maintaining alternative models reduces the risk of committing too early to one explanation. When evidence is incomplete, several causes may plausibly explain what is happening. A technician might consider a sensor fault, a communication failure, or a genuine equipment problem; a project manager might consider a resource shortage, unclear ownership, or an unrealistic schedule. Listing alternatives encourages the person to look for evidence that distinguishes them. It also reduces confirmation bias because the person is not simply collecting support for the first explanation. Alternative models should not become an excuse for indecision. The aim is to identify the most consequential uncertainties, gather discriminating evidence, and choose a response proportionate to the risk while remaining ready to revise the decision.
Study instruction:
- For a current problem, write three plausible explanations and identify the observation that would best distinguish among them.
Key point:
- Alternative models protect against premature certainty.
- The best next step is often the one that most clearly separates competing explanations.
Source links:
- NASA: Flight Cognition Laboratory
23. Q: What is the main contrast between the Air France Flight 447 and Qantas Flight 32 examples?
A: Show answer
The chapter contrasts two emergencies involving highly automated aircraft but different patterns of preparation, attention, and crew interaction. In its account of Flight 447, the pilots were confronted with unreliable airspeed information and an unexpected change in automation; the crew struggled to build a coherent picture of the situation and became absorbed in separate cues. In the Qantas Flight 32 account, Captain Richard de Crespigny had already encouraged the crew to imagine possible failures, agree that members should challenge one another, and consider where attention should be directed. When a severe engine failure damaged multiple aircraft systems, the crew worked through the situation, reconsidered a proposed action, and simplified the mental model as information accumulated. The comparison is not proof that preparation alone determines outcomes. The incidents had different technical conditions, and the Qantas crew also relied on expertise, procedures, coordination, and available aircraft capabilities.
Study instruction:
- Compare the two cases using preparation, information quality, attention allocation, crew communication, model revision, and outcome; avoid attributing either event to one factor alone.
Key point:
- Preparation and shared challenge can improve the conditions for sound judgment under pressure.
- Complex safety outcomes require analysis of both human and technical factors.
Source links:
- BEA: Air France Flight AF447 investigation
- ATSB: Qantas Flight QF32 investigation
24. Q: How did Captain de Crespigny's preflight questions prepare the Qantas Flight 32 crew?
A: Show answer
Before the flight, de Crespigny asked crew members to imagine what they would do if an engine failed and where they would look first. He also made clear that each person had a responsibility to disagree if the captain missed something. These questions encouraged the crew to form a shared picture of possible abnormal situations, rehearse attention priorities, and establish expectations for speaking up. The value was not that the crew could predict the exact failure. It was that they had practiced thinking about how a crisis might unfold and how they would coordinate. The chapter's example shows how scenario preparation can reduce the cognitive effort needed to begin responding when an unexpected event occurs. In any organization, pre-mortems and contingency discussions can serve a similar purpose when they are realistic and connected to actual responsibilities.
Study instruction:
- Conduct a short pre-mortem for an important project by asking what could go wrong, what early signs would appear, and who should raise each concern.
Key point:
- Scenario rehearsal prepares attention and communication before time pressure begins.
- Teams should explicitly authorize respectful challenge, including challenge to senior decision-makers.

- ATSB: Qantas Flight QF32 investigation
- FAA AC 120-71B: Standard Operating Procedures and Pilot Monitoring Duties
25. Q: Why was shared monitoring important during the Qantas Flight 32 emergency?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes the Qantas crew as distributing observation and challenge rather than allowing everyone to follow the same cue. De Crespigny asked a colleague to look in a different direction if the others were all looking at the same place. This is a practical defense against collective fixation: when several people attend to one display or explanation, another person can notice a neglected aspect of the situation. Shared monitoring also depends on clear communication, agreed roles, and permission to interrupt when a serious concern arises. The official ATSB investigation confirms the aircraft sustained extensive damage after an uncontained engine failure and that the crew managed the resulting system failures before returning safely to Singapore. The successful outcome should be understood as a coordinated operational response, not as the result of one mental technique alone.
Study instruction:
- In a team process, assign complementary review responsibilities and specify which person must independently challenge the main operator's assumptions.
Key point:
- Deliberately varied attention can reduce the risk that everyone misses the same signal.
- Shared monitoring works only when roles and communication expectations are clear.
Source links:
- ATSB: Qantas Flight QF32 investigation
- FAA AC 120-71B: Pilot Monitoring Duties
26. Q: Why did de Crespigny simplify the damaged Airbus into a mental picture of a Cessna?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes de Crespigny using the familiar basic structure of a simpler aircraft to reduce the complexity of the damaged A380 situation. The Airbus had many failed or impaired systems, and its computer messages created a large volume of information. Imagining the aircraft in terms of basic components such as fuel, controls, brakes, and landing gear helped him focus on the functions that remained available and the immediate problem of returning to land. The analogy did not make the A380 technically equivalent to a small aircraft, nor did it replace aircraft-specific procedures or calculations. Its value was cognitive simplification: it gave the captain a manageable representation of the problem while the crew continued to use expertise and operational information. Simplification is useful only when it preserves the constraints that matter.
Study instruction:
- Take a complex process and create a simplified model that retains its essential components, constraints, and failure points without pretending that important differences have disappeared.
Key point:
- A simpler model can reduce information overload and clarify the next decision.
- Simplification must preserve critical technical and operational constraints.
Source links:
- ATSB: Qantas Flight QF32 investigation
- NASA: Flight Cognition Laboratory
27. Q: What does the instruction to focus on what is still working teach about managing a crisis?
A: Show answer
During a crisis, attention can become consumed by failures, alarms, and missing capabilities. The chapter shows de Crespigny redirecting the crew toward systems that remained operational, such as available hydraulic functions, power, wheels, and braking capability. This helped the crew identify realistic options rather than repeatedly reviewing every failure. In business operations, a similar approach is to distinguish unavailable resources from capabilities that remain, identify minimum viable operations, and protect the functions needed to stabilize the situation. This does not mean ignoring failed systems or minimizing danger. Failed components must still be understood and managed. The principle is to balance diagnosis of what is wrong with a clear inventory of what can still be used safely and effectively.
Study instruction:
- For a process disruption, prepare two lists: failed or unavailable capabilities, and verified capabilities that remain usable; then identify the safe options supported by the second list.
Key point:
- Crisis decisions improve when people identify both failures and remaining capabilities.
- A remaining capability should be verified before it is treated as available.

- ATSB: Qantas Flight QF32 investigation
28. Q: How should people prioritize information when many alarms, messages, or problems appear at once?
A: Show answer
Start by identifying threats to life, safety, legal compliance, critical operations, or irreversible loss. Separate information that requires immediate action from information that can be investigated later. Confirm whether multiple alerts arise from one underlying failure, because treating every message as an independent problem can overload attention. Assign roles so that one person maintains the overall situation picture while others investigate specific issues. Use approved checklists and escalation procedures, and communicate which actions have been completed and which remain pending. In an office, the same logic means distinguishing a blocked payroll run from a routine report-format issue, or a suspected fraud indicator from an ordinary data-quality exception. Prioritization should be explicit and revisited as conditions change; urgency should not be based solely on the loudest notification.
Study instruction:
- Create a priority matrix for a realistic disruption, classifying each issue by consequence, time sensitivity, reversibility, and required owner.
Key point:
- The loudest or newest alert is not automatically the highest priority.
- Prioritization must be based on consequence and updated as evidence changes.
Source links:
- FAA ACT ARC Recommendation 20-1
- NASA: Flight Cognition Laboratory
29. Q: What is the proper relationship between checklists, automated prompts, and independent judgment?
A: Show answer
Checklists and automated prompts help people remember required steps, standardize responses, and access information quickly. They are valuable safeguards, especially when workload is high. However, a checklist cannot anticipate every context, and an automated prompt may be based on incomplete or misleading information. Operators should understand the purpose of the procedure, verify that it applies to the current situation, and monitor the result of following it. If an instruction appears inconsistent with the observed condition, personnel should use the approved procedure for resolving that discrepancy rather than silently improvising or blindly complying. The NASA Flight Cognition Laboratory notes that emergency procedures must be designed for high workload and human performance limitations. The practical lesson is to use checklists as structured support for judgment, not as a substitute for situational awareness or authorization.
Study instruction:
- Review one checklist or automated workflow and identify its scope, assumptions, exception process, and method for confirming that the action had the intended effect.
Key point:
- Checklists reduce omission risk but do not eliminate the need to interpret the situation.
- Apparent conflicts should be resolved through approved escalation and exception procedures.

- NASA: Flight Cognition Laboratory
- FAA AC 120-71B: Standard Operating Procedures
30. Q: What practical framework can help a person manage attention during a demanding task?
A: Show answer
A useful framework has five steps. First, define the objective and the critical condition that must be protected. Second, identify the information sources and cues that matter most. Third, allocate attention deliberately by deciding when to inspect each source and which activities must not interrupt the task. Fourth, compare observed results with the expected mental model and actively look for contradictions. Fifth, reassess priorities when the situation changes and communicate significant deviations. In a financial close, for example, the objective may be accurate and timely reporting; critical cues may include unreconciled balances, late journals, and unsupported adjustments. The framework should be adapted to the process and must not replace required policies or specialist procedures. Its purpose is to make attention management explicit rather than leaving it to habit.
Study instruction:
- Apply the five steps to one complex task and record the objective, critical cues, monitoring schedule, contradiction checks, and escalation trigger.
Key point:
- Deliberate attention management combines prioritization, monitoring, model checking, and communication.
- Reassessment is necessary whenever the situation changes materially.
Source links:
- FAA ACT ARC Recommendation 20-1
- NASA: Flight Cognition Laboratory
31. Q: How should a monitoring role be designed in a two-person or team-based process?
A: Show answer
The monitoring role should be defined around independent observation and challenge, not merely duplicate data entry. The monitor needs to know the objective, the critical indicators, the limits of acceptable performance, and the conditions that require escalation. The role should include checking the operator's actions against the expected result, watching for deviations, and communicating concerns early. It should also be clear when the monitor may interrupt, stop a step, or refer the matter to a higher authority. In an accounts-payable process, for example, the reviewer should independently confirm vendor identity, authorization, supporting documentation, and payment details rather than simply re-reading the preparer's summary. The FAA guidance on pilot-monitoring duties illustrates the importance of integrating monitoring responsibilities into formal procedures and training.
Study instruction:
- Write a responsibility statement for the monitor in one team process, including independent checks, communication duties, and escalation authority.
Key point:
- Independent monitoring is a control that must have defined scope and authority.
- A second person adds value by checking the underlying evidence, not by repeating the same assumption.
Source links:
- FAA AC 120-71B: Pilot Monitoring Duties
32. Q: Why is explicit challenge and response important when people work under pressure?
A: Show answer
Under pressure, people may assume that another person has noticed a problem, may hesitate to contradict a senior colleague, or may interpret silence as agreement. Explicit challenge and response makes important information visible. A team member states the concern, identifies the evidence, and asks for a clear response; the decision-maker acknowledges it and explains whether the plan will change or why it will remain. This reduces ambiguity about whether the message was heard. The chapter's Qantas example illustrates a captain who expected colleagues to disagree when they saw a risk. In other settings, a finance reviewer might challenge a journal entry that does not reconcile, and the preparer should respond with evidence rather than simply saying it has been checked. Challenge should be respectful, specific, and tied to the task or risk.
Study instruction:
- Practice a concise challenge statement using the structure: observed fact, potential consequence, requested verification, and response needed.
Key point:
- Important concerns should be acknowledged and resolved explicitly, not left as implied disagreement.
- A healthy team treats evidence-based challenge as part of responsible work.

- FAA AC 120-71B: Standard Operating Procedures and Pilot Monitoring Duties
- ATSB: Qantas Flight QF32 investigation
33. Q: What are warning signs that a person may be experiencing attentional capture or cognitive tunneling?
A: Show answer
Warning signs can include repeatedly looking at one display or document, missing expected checklist steps, failing to communicate, overlooking changes in the wider situation, repeating the same unsuccessful action, and being unable to explain how the current information fits together. The FAA ACT ARC Recommendation 20-1 identifies lack of communication, missed checklist items, and failure to verify flight modes as examples of potential attentional capture in aviation. In office work, comparable signs include refreshing one dashboard while ignoring source records, repeatedly editing one paragraph while missing a deadline, or responding to notifications without remembering the original priority. These signs should prompt a deliberate reset: pause if safe, restate the objective, scan the relevant indicators, ask what has changed, and involve another person when necessary.
Study instruction:
- Create a personal warning-sign checklist and identify the action you will take when two or more signs appear during important work.
Key point:
- Attentional capture often appears in behavior before the person recognizes it internally.
- Predefined warning signs make it easier to interrupt an unproductive focus pattern.
Source links:
- FAA ACT ARC Recommendation 20-1
34. Q: What should someone do when an unexpected interruption abruptly demands attention?
A: Show answer
The person should first determine whether the interruption is urgent and whether it changes the priority of the current task. If it is not urgent, record it and return to the planned activity at an appropriate stopping point. If it is urgent, establish the new objective, identify what information is known and unknown, and deliberately shift attention rather than reacting to the first visible cue. Before resuming the original task, reconstruct where the work stopped and check whether any step was left incomplete. This is particularly important when a person is interrupted during a multi-step process, because resumption errors can occur when the individual forgets an intended action. In safety-critical work, the response must follow the applicable interruption and handover procedures. The general principle is to make task switching visible and controlled.
Study instruction:
- Design a brief interruption protocol for your work that records the current step, urgency of the new request, and safe point for resumption.
Key point:
- An interruption should trigger a deliberate reprioritization, not an uncontrolled shift of attention.
- Resumption checks reduce the risk of forgotten or duplicated steps.
Source links:
- NASA: Flight Cognition Laboratory
35. Q: Why is multitasking often less effective than people expect?
A: Show answer
Many activities described as multitasking actually involve switching attention between tasks rather than processing each one fully at the same time. Each switch can require the person to reconstruct context, remember the next step, and decide what deserves attention. This creates time costs and increases the chance of omission, especially when tasks are complex or have safety, financial, or reputational consequences. The chapter links the modern use of automated systems and multiple digital communications with a greater need to manage focus. The NASA Flight Cognition Laboratory also describes how concurrent tasks, interruptions, and distractions can cause skilled operators to forget steps or lose track of tasks. A practical approach is to batch similar low-risk activities, protect uninterrupted periods for complex work, and use written reminders for tasks that must be resumed later.
Study instruction:
- Track one work period and record each task switch, the reason for it, and the time or errors associated with resuming the original task.
Key point:
- Frequent switching can create hidden costs even when a person feels busy and productive.
- Protect complex tasks from unnecessary interruption and use explicit resumption cues.
Source links:
- NASA: Flight Cognition Laboratory
36. Q: How can the chapter's mental-model approach improve an ordinary workday?
A: Show answer
Before beginning the day, imagine the main activities in sequence and identify what each one is intended to accomplish. Anticipate the information, people, and decisions likely to be needed. During the day, compare events with that expectation and notice unusual delays, missing information, or changes in other people's behavior. At the end, review what differed from the original picture and update the model for tomorrow. This is not a rigid schedule or an attempt to predict every event. It is a way to make work more deliberate and to recognize deviations sooner. For example, a finance officer might anticipate a payment review, identify the evidence required, and notice that a supplier's bank details have changed unexpectedly. The model helps direct attention to the discrepancy before a payment is released.
Study instruction:
- Spend five minutes forecasting tomorrow's three most important tasks, then review which expectations were accurate and which should be revised.
Key point:
- A brief forecast prepares attention for the work and deviations likely to matter.
- Reviewing the difference between expectation and reality improves future models.
Source links:
- NASA: Flight Cognition Laboratory
37. Q: How can mental models help someone prepare for an important meeting?
A: Show answer
A meeting model should include the purpose, the decision required, the participants' responsibilities, the evidence likely to be discussed, and the questions that could change the outcome. Imagine how the conversation may begin, what objections may arise, and which facts would support or weaken the proposal. This preparation helps the participant listen for relevant signals instead of trying to invent a response at the moment a difficult question is asked. It also makes it easier to notice when the meeting moves away from its objective. The model should not become a script that prevents listening. If participants introduce new evidence or a concern that was not anticipated, the model must be updated. A good meeting model therefore prepares both a direction and a willingness to revise it.
Study instruction:
- Prepare a one-page meeting brief with the intended decision, key facts, likely objections, unanswered questions, and conditions that would change your recommendation.
Key point:
- Anticipation improves readiness without requiring a fixed script.
- Effective participants update their model when the discussion produces new information.
Source links:
- NASA: Flight Cognition Laboratory
38. Q: How can a project manager use mental models to detect delivery problems earlier?
A: Show answer
A project manager can model the chain of dependencies that must work for delivery to remain on schedule. This includes inputs, responsible owners, approval points, external suppliers, critical milestones, and assumptions about capacity. The manager should identify leading indicators such as late decisions, incomplete specifications, unresolved risks, or repeated handoff failures. When actual progress differs from the model, the manager should determine whether the plan was unrealistic, a dependency has changed, or a new constraint has emerged. The model can also help the manager ask better questions in status meetings: what changed, what is blocked, what evidence supports the current forecast, and what decision is needed now? This turns project monitoring from passive collection of percentages into active interpretation of the work system.
Study instruction:
- Draw a dependency map for one project and mark the earliest observable signals that would indicate the delivery model is no longer accurate.
Key point:
- A project model connects milestones to the dependencies and assumptions that make them possible.
- Early deviations should trigger investigation and adjustment before they become missed deadlines.
Source links:
- NASA: Flight Cognition Laboratory
39. Q: What did the chapter's MIT recruiting-firm study suggest about how highly productive employees build mental models?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes economists and a sociologist who examined data from a recruiting firm, including project assignments, calendars, profit-and-loss information, and a large volume of email. The most productive employees were not simply taking on the greatest number of projects or repeating only familiar tasks. They tended to limit the number of simultaneous projects, engage in work that exposed them to new colleagues and skills, join some projects early, and generate many explanations about what they observed. They discussed theories, rehearsed future conversations, and connected information from different parts of the organization. The chapter interprets these behaviors as repeated construction of mental models. The reported relationship is specific to the firm and study described; it should not be treated as proof that the same number of projects or behavior will maximize productivity in every organization.
Study instruction:
- Identify which parts of your work encourage you to connect information and generate explanations, and which activities merely consume time without improving understanding.
Key point:
- The chapter links productive work with deliberate sense-making and active model-building.
- Findings from one organization should be applied as hypotheses, not universal productivity rules.
Source links:
- NASA: Flight Cognition Laboratory
40. Q: Why did the chapter's most productive employees appear to limit the number of projects they handled at once?
A: Show answer
The chapter reports that the employees identified as superstars worked on roughly five projects at a time, while some colleagues handled ten or twelve. The proposed explanation was not that five is a universal optimum. Rather, the high performers invested time in projects that required new relationships, new abilities, and deeper understanding. These activities created additional learning and information costs, so taking on too many projects would have diluted the attention needed to benefit from them. The practical lesson is to evaluate workload by cognitive demand and strategic value, not just by the number of assignments. A person with fewer projects may be more productive if those projects create meaningful outcomes and allow enough time for analysis, collaboration, and follow-through. Capacity should be assessed in the context of the role and the work.
Study instruction:
- Review your active commitments and classify each by strategic value, learning demand, time requirement, and opportunity cost before accepting another project.
Key point:
- More simultaneous assignments do not automatically produce more valuable output.
- The right workload depends on complexity, learning demands, and available attention.
Source links:
- NASA: Flight Cognition Laboratory
41. Q: Why can joining an early-stage project provide a learning advantage?
A: Show answer
The chapter explains that early-stage projects can expose participants to information that is less visible once a project is established. People may see initial assumptions, emerging customer needs, early design choices, and the reasoning behind decisions. They can also meet colleagues from different functions and learn about new markets or capabilities before information becomes widely distributed. This exposure can help them build more accurate mental models of the organization and identify opportunities that are harder to see later. However, early-stage projects also carry uncertainty and a higher possibility of failure. Joining them is not automatically wise; the person should consider strategic fit, available capacity, sponsorship, and the cost of experimentation. The lesson is to seek information-rich work deliberately while managing the risks of uncertainty.
Study instruction:
- Identify one early-stage initiative that could broaden your understanding, then assess its learning value, uncertainty, time commitment, and strategic relevance.
Key point:
- Early participation can provide access to emerging information and relationships.
- Learning opportunities must be balanced against project risk and personal capacity.
Source links:
- NASA: Flight Cognition Laboratory
42. Q: How does storytelling help people connect information and remember experience?
A: Show answer
A coherent story links events, decisions, causes, and consequences into a structure that is easier to reason about than a disconnected list of details. When people narrate what happened, they are more likely to ask why an event occurred, how it relates to earlier information, and what may happen next. This can deepen understanding and make it easier to retrieve relevant experience when a similar situation appears. The chapter describes productive employees as frequently reconstructing conversations and explaining their theories to colleagues. Storytelling should still distinguish observed facts from inferred motives. A persuasive narrative can be wrong if it omits evidence or ignores alternative explanations. The best practice is to use stories to organize information and then test them against records, other perspectives, and actual outcomes.
Study instruction:
- Turn a recent work event into a short evidence-based narrative that separates what happened, what you inferred, what remains uncertain, and what you learned.
Key point:
- Narratives can connect details into a usable explanation of experience.
- A compelling story must still be checked against evidence and alternative accounts.
Source links:
- NASA: Flight Cognition Laboratory
43. Q: What daily routine can help someone strengthen the habit of building mental models?
A: Show answer
A practical routine can be organized around three moments. Before an important task, forecast the objective, sequence, people, and likely obstacles. During the task, narrate what is changing and compare actual events with the expected picture. After the task, review what surprised you, which assumptions were wrong, and what should be remembered for next time. The routine should be brief enough to sustain and focused on work that matters. It is not necessary to write a long diary for every activity. A short note, a quick verbal explanation, or a sketch of the process may be sufficient. Over time, this practice builds a library of tested models that can improve preparation and help people notice unusual details sooner.
Study instruction:
- Use a before-during-after reflection for one important activity each day for a week and record one model revision per day.
Key point:
- Mental-model skill grows through repeated forecasting, observation, and review.
- Small, consistent practice is more useful than occasional elaborate planning.
Source links:
- NASA: Flight Cognition Laboratory
44. Q: How can simulation and rehearsal improve performance in unexpected situations?
A: Show answer
Simulation allows people to practice recognizing cues, coordinating roles, and selecting actions before a real event creates high pressure. It can expose weaknesses in procedures, assumptions, communication, and the mental models used by participants. Rehearsal is especially valuable when the event is rare but the consequences are severe, because people may not have many opportunities to learn through real experience. The chapter's Qantas example describes a captain who routinely asked the crew to imagine possible failures before departure. NASA's research on emergency and abnormal situations emphasizes the challenge of simultaneous problems, ambiguous cues, and high workload. Effective simulation should therefore include realistic uncertainty and multiple interacting demands, followed by a review of what participants noticed, missed, and assumed. It should not be limited to memorizing one scripted sequence.
Study instruction:
- Design a short simulation for a low-frequency, high-impact event and define the cues, roles, decision points, and debrief questions.
Key point:
- Rehearsal prepares people to recognize and coordinate around abnormal conditions.
- Scenarios should test judgment and adaptation, not only memorized steps.
Source links:
- NASA: Flight Cognition Laboratory
- ATSB: Qantas Flight QF32 investigation
45. Q: What makes a useful what-if exercise different from simply imagining worst-case scenarios?
A: Show answer
A useful what-if exercise links a plausible event to observable warning signs, decisions, responsibilities, and available options. It does not merely create frightening possibilities. Participants should ask what could change, how they would recognize the change, which assumptions might fail, who needs to be informed, and what procedure governs the response. The exercise should also include constraints and trade-offs so that participants cannot solve every problem by assuming unlimited time or resources. For example, a project team might consider a critical supplier failing shortly before a deadline and identify the earliest evidence of disruption, the decision owner, alternative suppliers, and contractual restrictions. A good exercise produces practical preparedness and exposes gaps in the current plan. It should be updated as risks, resources, and procedures change.
Study instruction:
- Convert one worst-case concern into a structured scenario with triggers, owners, decision points, constraints, and a review date.
Key point:
- Scenario planning is useful when it produces observable triggers and actionable responses.
- Rehearsal should expose assumptions and resource limits, not reward unrealistic optimism.
Source links:
- NASA: Flight Cognition Laboratory
46. Q: How can an after-action review improve a person's or team's mental models?
A: Show answer
An after-action review compares what participants expected to happen with what actually happened. The review should establish the objective, reconstruct the sequence using evidence, identify decisions and assumptions, examine which cues were noticed or missed, and determine why the result differed from the expectation. It should distinguish individual actions from system conditions such as unclear roles, poor interfaces, insufficient information, or conflicting priorities. The purpose is learning, not automatic blame. Corrective actions should be specific, assigned to an owner, and checked later to determine whether they changed the process. A review is most useful when it produces a revised model that can guide future decisions. It is less useful when it becomes a vague discussion about being more careful or communicating better.
Study instruction:
- Conduct an after-action review of one completed task using expected outcome, actual sequence, missed cues, contributing conditions, revised model, and assigned actions.
Key point:
- Comparing expectation with evidence turns experience into improved understanding.
- Learning requires concrete changes and follow-up, not only discussion.
Source links:
- NASA: Flight Cognition Laboratory
47. Q: What are the limitations of mental models, and how can overconfidence be avoided?
A: Show answer
Mental models simplify reality, so they inevitably omit some details. A model built from limited experience may not apply to a new environment, and a model that once worked may become outdated as technology, people, or conditions change. People may also mistake a plausible narrative for a verified explanation, become attached to an earlier prediction, or overlook evidence that challenges their assumptions. Overconfidence can be reduced by stating uncertainties, identifying disconfirming evidence, asking another person to challenge the model, and checking important conclusions against reliable data or formal procedures. In high-risk settings, the model must remain subordinate to approved instructions and qualified judgment. The objective is not to build a perfect picture of every possibility but to maintain a useful, revisable understanding of the situation.
Study instruction:
- Review one decision you made with confidence and identify what information was missing, what alternative explanation was possible, and what would have changed your conclusion.
Key point:
- Every mental model is incomplete and may become outdated.
- Explicit uncertainty and independent challenge reduce the risk of overconfidence.
Source links:
- NASA: Flight Cognition Laboratory
48. Q: How can a team use different perspectives to improve its shared mental model?
A: Show answer
Team members often hold different information because they have different roles, experience, expertise, and access to events. A shared mental model develops when people exchange those perspectives and reach a sufficiently common understanding of the objective, system state, responsibilities, and likely next steps. Agreement should not mean that everyone must think identically. Dissenting views can reveal gaps in the dominant explanation and prevent the group from becoming collectively fixated. Leaders can ask each function to describe what it sees, invite someone to challenge the main assumption, and summarize the agreed facts separately from unresolved questions. In complex work, the team should record decisions and changes so that members do not act on incompatible versions of the situation.
Study instruction:
- Ask three people with different roles to describe the same process and compare what each notices, assumes, and considers risky.
Key point:
- A shared model is built by integrating distinct information, not suppressing differences.
- Structured dissent helps identify blind spots in the team's common explanation.
Source links:
- NASA: Flight Cognition Laboratory
- FAA AC 120-71B: Pilot Monitoring Duties
49. Q: How can an organization evaluate whether attention-management and mental-model practices are improving performance?
A: Show answer
Evaluation should begin with a clear problem and a baseline. Relevant indicators may include the frequency of missed steps, repeated errors, delayed escalation, rework, unexpected interruptions, unresolved exceptions, or decisions that must be reversed because important information was overlooked. Qualitative evidence is also important: ask whether staff can explain the process, identify critical cues, describe what would change their view, and raise concerns when the model no longer fits. Avoid treating one metric as conclusive. A higher number of reported problems may indicate improved detection rather than worsening performance, while fewer reported problems may reflect silence rather than better control. Compare results over time and across similar tasks, then investigate whether changes in workload, staffing, technology, or procedures explain the outcome.
Study instruction:
- Define two behavioral indicators and two outcome indicators for one process, establish a baseline, and specify how context will be considered when interpreting results.
Key point:
- Effective evaluation combines observable behavior, process quality, and actual outcomes.
- Metrics must be interpreted in context rather than treated as proof by themselves.
Source links:
- FAA ACT ARC Recommendation 20-1
- NASA: Flight Cognition Laboratory
50. Q: What integrated practical lessons should a learner take from Chapter 3 about focus and mental models?
A: Show answer
The chapter's central lessons can be translated into a repeatable discipline. Before important work, define the objective and build a realistic picture of how the task or system should operate. Anticipate likely changes and identify the signals that would indicate a problem. During the work, allocate attention deliberately, avoid unnecessary switching, and monitor more than the most obvious display or message. When something unexpected happens, do not automatically apply the first familiar response; establish what is known, check the current conditions, use the applicable procedure, and seek independent challenge where appropriate. Compare the observed situation with the mental model, consider alternative explanations, and revise the model when evidence requires it. Afterward, review the outcome and capture lessons for the next task. These habits do not eliminate uncertainty or human error, but they improve the quality of attention, interpretation, and coordinated action.
Study instruction:
- Apply the complete cycle to one real task: forecast, prioritize, monitor, challenge assumptions, respond through the proper procedure, review, and update the model.
Key point:
- Better focus comes from deliberate attention supported by tested and revisable mental models.
- Preparation, independent monitoring, procedural discipline, and learning from outcomes work together.
Source links:
- BEA: Air France Flight AF447 investigation
- ATSB: Qantas Flight QF32 investigation
- FAA ACT ARC Recommendation 20-1
- NASA: Flight Cognition Laboratory
