Table 4
Goal Setting: SMART Plans, Stretch Ambitions, and Decision Discipline
1. Q: What is the central lesson of Chapter 4 about effective goal setting?
A: Show answer
Effective goal setting requires both a meaningful ambition and a disciplined route for acting on it. SMART-style goals translate intentions into specific actions, measures, feasible commitments, and deadlines. Stretch goals prevent the system from shrinking attention to only safe, incremental tasks. The chapter's central warning is that neither method is sufficient by itself: an ambitious goal can overwhelm people if there is no workable path, while a perfectly specified plan can become a polished way to pursue something trivial. The Yom Kippur War case adds a further safeguard: even a well-structured goal must be reconsidered when evidence changes or the underlying objective no longer makes sense.
Study instruction:
- Summarize the chapter as a three-part model: choose a consequential ambition, translate it into workable steps, and periodically test whether the ambition remains valid.
Key point:
- Combine aspiration, executable planning, and continuing judgment.
- Do not confuse progress toward a target with progress toward the right outcome.
Source links:
- Locke and Latham: Building a Practically Useful Theory of Goal Setting
- Sitkin et al.: The Paradox of Stretch Goals
2. Q: What does goal setting do that a vague intention does not?
A: Show answer
A goal gives attention a direction and makes the desired result sufficiently clear to guide effort, persistence, and strategy. “Do your best” leaves the performer to decide what counts as enough, how progress will be judged, and when to change approach. A defined target can help a person recognize the gap between current performance and the desired result, select relevant actions, and use feedback to adjust. Goal setting is not magic: a target cannot create missing skill, resources, authority, or time. Its value is that it organizes available effort around an outcome that can be examined and improved rather than relying on an unstructured wish.
Study instruction:
- Rewrite one vague intention as an observable outcome, then list the actions and evidence that would show whether progress is occurring.
Key point:
- Goals direct attention and organize effort.
- A target is useful only when people can act on it and assess progress.
Source links:
- Locke and Latham: Building a Practically Useful Theory of Goal Setting
3. Q: What is the need for cognitive closure, and why does it matter when setting goals?
A: Show answer
The need for cognitive closure is a desire for a definite answer rather than continued ambiguity. It can make people decisive, organized, and willing to commit, but it can also make them uncomfortable with uncertainty and alternative explanations. In the chapter, this trait helps explain why Eli Zeira valued crisp intelligence estimates and why he resisted reopening a conclusion after adopting it. In goal setting, closure can help a person stop debating and begin work; the danger is treating the emotional relief of having an answer as evidence that the answer is correct. Good goal management therefore needs both commitment and a disciplined willingness to revisit assumptions.
Study instruction:
- Identify a decision you tend to settle quickly and write down what new evidence would justify reopening it.
Key point:
- Closure can support action but can also suppress reconsideration.
- Decisiveness is not the same as accuracy.
Source links:
- Kruglanski and Webster: Motivated Closing of the Mind
4. Q: How can a high need for closure help performance?
A: Show answer
A reasonable preference for closure can reduce unnecessary hesitation, repeated second-guessing, and unproductive debate. Once a decision meets an acceptable standard, a person can commit resources, coordinate with others, and move to the next task. This can be valuable in time-sensitive work, routine operations, or activities that require sustained concentration. The chapter notes that decisive people may be self-disciplined and perceived as leaders, and that some skilled performers use closure to focus on the current problem rather than dwell on earlier mistakes. The benefit depends on the decision being sufficiently informed and the environment being stable enough for commitment to remain sensible.
Study instruction:
- Review a decision that benefited from timely commitment and identify the minimum evidence that made commitment reasonable.
Key point:
- Closure can convert analysis into action.
- Commit after adequate evaluation, not merely to relieve discomfort.
Source links:
- Kruglanski and Webster: Motivated Closing of the Mind
- Locke and Latham: Building a Practically Useful Theory of Goal Setting
5. Q: What are the main risks of an excessive need for cognitive closure?
A: Show answer
Excessive closure can cause people to seize on an early explanation and freeze on it even when contradictory evidence appears. They may dismiss dissent, reinterpret warning signs to fit their existing view, or treat further discussion as a threat to decisiveness. Under pressure, this can create premature judgment, rigidity, overconfidence, and poor cooperation. The chapter illustrates the danger through Zeira's repeated reliance on the assumption that Egypt and Syria would not attack without particular military capabilities. Reports of troop movements, equipment, and evacuations were interpreted through that fixed concept rather than used to test it. The practical risk is not simply making a wrong decision; it is creating a culture in which correction becomes difficult.
Study instruction:
- For a current project, list three assumptions that could become fixed and specify an observable warning that would challenge each one.
Key point:
- A fixed conclusion can turn new information into something to explain away.
- Build explicit opportunities to challenge assumptions.
Source links:
- Kruglanski and Webster: Motivated Closing of the Mind
- Israel Ministry of Defense Archives: Agranat Committee
6. Q: What is the difference between “seizing” and “freezing” in cognitive closure?
A: Show answer
Seizing is the impulse to adopt a judgment or goal once it appears acceptable enough to end uncertainty and begin acting. Freezing is the subsequent tendency to hold that judgment in place and resist information that could require revision. Seizing can be useful because endless deliberation prevents action. Freezing becomes dangerous when commitment is treated as irreversible or when people stop distinguishing a working hypothesis from an established fact. In a healthy process, a team can seize a provisional direction while keeping review criteria, alternative explanations, and escalation routes open. The chapter's lesson is to preserve the ability to change course without turning every decision into endless debate.
Study instruction:
- Take a recent decision and mark the point at which you seized on an option; then identify whether you had a defined process for revisiting it.
Key point:
- Seizing enables commitment; freezing blocks correction.
- Treat important decisions as revisable when evidence warrants it.
Source links:
- Kruglanski and Webster: Motivated Closing of the Mind
7. Q: What goal did Eli Zeira initially set for Israel's military intelligence directorate?
A: Show answer
Zeira said his task was to provide decision makers with an estimate as clear and sharp as possible and to raise alarms only when the risk of war was real. This responded to frustration with inconsistent forecasts that repeatedly caused public anxiety, military preparations, and reversals. The goal had a legitimate operational concern: warnings that are too frequent or poorly calibrated can impose costs on reservists, families, the economy, and public confidence. The failure described in the chapter was not that clarity was inherently wrong. It was that the desire to reduce unnecessary alarms became so dominant that the organization discounted evidence that did not fit its preferred explanation and lost sight of the higher purpose of protecting the country.
Study instruction:
- Distinguish Zeira's stated operational objective from the higher-level mission it was supposed to serve.
Key point:
- A measurable operational aim must remain subordinate to the mission it supports.
- Reducing false alarms cannot justify ignoring credible danger.
Source links:
- Israel Ministry of Defense Archives: Agranat Committee
- Israel Ministry of Defense Archives: The Agranat Commission Report
8. Q: Why was Zeira's intelligence “concept” an inadequate basis for judging the risk of attack?
A: Show answer
The concept assumed that Egypt and Syria would not attack until they possessed specified air and missile capabilities. It offered a clear decision rule, but it was too narrow to capture the possibility that an adversary might accept military disadvantages, use surprise, or pursue a different operational strategy. Once the rule became embedded in the directorate's culture, analysts treated it as a filter rather than a hypothesis to test. A useful framework should help organize evidence without excluding relevant possibilities. The case demonstrates why assumptions need explicit review, why alternative scenarios matter, and why indicators should be assessed in combination rather than rejected because one expected condition is absent.
Study instruction:
- Write the assumptions behind a decision rule and construct one plausible scenario in which the rule would fail.
Key point:
- A clear model can still be incomplete.
- Test a framework against disconfirming evidence and alternative scenarios.
Source links:
- Israel Ministry of Defense Archives: Agranat Committee
- Kruglanski and Webster: Motivated Closing of the Mind
9. Q: How should a decision maker respond when warning indicators conflict with an established forecast?
A: Show answer
The decision maker should separate the forecast from the evidence, identify which observations are new, and ask whether the assumptions behind the forecast still hold. Relevant indicators should be assessed for reliability, timing, independence, and combined significance. The reviewer should invite analysts with different interpretations to explain their reasoning, record unresolved uncertainty, and define what additional evidence would change the assessment. A forecast should be updated when the weight of evidence changes, even if the update is inconvenient or politically costly. In the chapter, reports of convoys, minefield removal, bridging equipment, Soviet evacuations, and force concentrations were warning indicators that deserved a fresh assessment rather than automatic assimilation into the old concept.
Study instruction:
- Create a simple warning-indicator log with source, date, reliability, implication, alternative explanation, and escalation threshold.
Key point:
- New evidence should test the forecast, not merely be fitted to it.
- Record uncertainty and escalation triggers before a crisis.
Source links:
- Israel Ministry of Defense Archives: Agranat Committee
- Israel Ministry of Defense Archives: The Agranat Commission Report
10. Q: Why is dissent important in high-stakes goal and decision processes?
A: Show answer
Dissent exposes assumptions that a cohesive group may otherwise leave untested. Analysts or employees who see a different risk, constraint, or opportunity can reveal blind spots before they become costly failures. Dissent is useful only when people can express it without humiliation or retaliation and when decision makers evaluate the substance rather than the status of the speaker. The chapter describes how Zeira's intolerance for prolonged debate and his treatment of officers who disagreed discouraged alternative estimates. A disciplined process does not require accepting every objection; it requires hearing it, testing it, documenting the response, and preserving a channel for escalation when new evidence emerges.
Study instruction:
- In a team meeting, assign one person to present the strongest evidence against the preferred plan and record how the group responds.
Key point:
- Constructive dissent is a control against group error.
- Challenge ideas without punishing people for raising them.
Source links:
- Kruglanski and Webster: Motivated Closing of the Mind
- Israel Ministry of Defense Archives: Agranat Committee
11. Q: What does SMART mean in the goal-setting approach described in the chapter?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes SMART objectives as specific, measurable, achievable, realistic, and based on a timeline. Specificity defines the result or area of improvement. Measurement identifies an indicator of progress or completion. Achievability and realism test whether the objective can be pursued with available capability and resources. A timeline gives the work a time boundary and sequence. The criteria are intended to force managers to explain what they are trying to accomplish and how they will know whether it happened. They are not a substitute for deciding whether the objective is important, aligned with strategy, ethically appropriate, or worth the resources it consumes.
Study instruction:
- Choose one objective and annotate its specific result, measure, feasibility evidence, resource assumptions, and deadline.
Key point:
- SMART turns an intention into a testable plan.
- Strategic value must be checked in addition to technical completeness.
Source links:
- Locke and Latham: Building a Practically Useful Theory of Goal Setting
- George T. Doran: There’s a S.M.A.R.T. Way to Write Management’s Goals and Objectives
12. Q: Is there one universally accepted expansion of the SMART acronym?
A: Show answer
No. SMART has been adapted across organizations and publications, so the words represented by each letter are not completely uniform. The chapter uses specific, measurable, achievable, realistic, and timeline-based. George T. Doran's 1981 article used specific, measurable, assignable, realistic, and time-related. The difference matters because Doran explicitly emphasized who is responsible, while other versions emphasize feasibility or achievability. An organization should define the version it uses and ensure that important elements are not lost. Even when “assignable” is not part of the acronym, ownership must still be clear; even when a measure is qualitative, the objective should specify what evidence will indicate progress.
Study instruction:
- Compare the chapter's SMART wording with Doran's original formulation and add an explicit owner field to your own goal template.
Key point:
- SMART is a flexible framework, not a single fixed legal or scientific standard.
- Define the terms locally and retain accountability and evidence.
Source links:
- George T. Doran: There’s a S.M.A.R.T. Way to Write Management’s Goals and Objectives
13. Q: How can a goal be made sufficiently specific?
A: Show answer
A specific goal identifies the outcome, scope, relevant population or process, and the change being sought. “Improve service” is too broad because it does not identify which service, whose experience, or what improvement means. A more specific formulation might identify the service, the stage of delivery, and the expected result, while leaving the exact method open for problem solving. Specificity should clarify the destination without prematurely prescribing every operational detail. If a goal is complex, it may need several sub-objectives or milestones. The objective should also distinguish the desired outcome from activities that merely support it, because completing activities does not necessarily produce the intended result.
Study instruction:
- Rewrite a broad goal so that a person unfamiliar with the project can identify the intended outcome and scope without guessing.
Key point:
- Specify the result, not just the activity.
- Avoid wording that can be interpreted in several incompatible ways.
Source links:
- George T. Doran: There’s a S.M.A.R.T. Way to Write Management’s Goals and Objectives
- Locke and Latham: Building a Practically Useful Theory of Goal Setting
14. Q: What makes a goal measurable without encouraging the wrong behavior?
A: Show answer
A measure should provide credible evidence of progress toward the intended outcome, not merely count whatever is easiest to count. It may be quantitative, such as time, error rate, cost, or completion rate, or qualitative, such as independently assessed quality against defined criteria. A good measurement design includes a baseline, a target, a data source, a frequency, and a responsible reviewer. It should also include balancing indicators where optimizing one number could damage another dimension. For example, reducing processing time should not be considered success if error rates or client harm increase. The chapter's GE examples show how a formally measurable objective can still be trivial or counterproductive if the metric is poorly connected to value.
Study instruction:
- For one target, define the baseline, outcome measure, data source, review frequency, and one balancing measure that could reveal unintended harm.
Key point:
- Measure the outcome that matters, not just the activity that is easy to count.
- Pair target metrics with quality and risk checks.
Source links:
- Locke and Latham: Building a Practically Useful Theory of Goal Setting
- George T. Doran: There’s a S.M.A.R.T. Way to Write Management’s Goals and Objectives
15. Q: How should achievability be assessed before a goal is approved?
A: Show answer
Achievability should be assessed against the skills, authority, resources, dependencies, and time available to the people responsible. The assessment should identify the assumptions that make the target plausible and the capability gaps that must be addressed. If a target requires new technology, training, approvals, or cooperation from another team, those dependencies belong in the plan rather than being treated as invisible. Achievable does not mean easy; a difficult objective can be achievable when the path is understood and the commitment is credible. If no plausible path exists, the goal may need staged experiments, revised scope, additional resources, or a different target rather than an unsupported promise.
Study instruction:
- Prepare a feasibility check covering capability, resources, decision rights, dependencies, and the main assumptions behind the target.
Key point:
- Feasibility is a reasoned judgment, not optimism.
- Make capability gaps and dependencies visible before commitment.
Source links:
- Locke and Latham: Building a Practically Useful Theory of Goal Setting
- George T. Doran: There’s a S.M.A.R.T. Way to Write Management’s Goals and Objectives
16. Q: What is the difference between an achievable goal and a realistic goal?
A: Show answer
The terms overlap, but they can prompt different questions. Achievability asks whether the intended result can be reached through a plausible sequence of actions. Realism asks whether the target fits the actual operating conditions, including capacity, constraints, competing priorities, uncertainty, and the consequences of failure. A goal might be technically achievable but unrealistic within the proposed deadline or budget. Conversely, a realistic aspiration may still require new skills or a stretch in effort. Teams should state the assumptions and trade-offs behind the assessment rather than using “realistic” as a synonym for “comfortable.” The chapter's GE examples show that realism should not be used to prevent all ambitious thinking, but feasibility must remain grounded.
Study instruction:
- Assess a goal twice: first for a plausible route to achievement, then for fit with current operating constraints and trade-offs.
Key point:
- Achievability concerns the path; realism concerns the context.
- Do not confuse realism with keeping targets unnecessarily modest.
Source links:
- George T. Doran: There’s a S.M.A.R.T. Way to Write Management’s Goals and Objectives
- Locke and Latham: Building a Practically Useful Theory of Goal Setting
17. Q: Why should a goal include a timeline?
A: Show answer
A timeline turns an open-ended intention into a commitment that can be sequenced and reviewed. It helps people decide what must happen first, when dependencies are needed, and whether progress is fast enough to meet the intended result. A useful timeline includes intermediate milestones for complex work, not only one final date. It should reflect lead times, approvals, seasonal conditions, and realistic capacity. Deadlines can create focus, but an arbitrary deadline may encourage shortcuts, concealment, or poor-quality work. The purpose is not to force every activity into an artificial date; it is to create a credible rhythm for action, feedback, and escalation when a milestone is missed.
Study instruction:
- Break a final deadline into milestones and specify what evidence must be available at each checkpoint.
Key point:
- Timelines make progress and delay visible.
- Milestones should support learning and quality, not only speed.
Source links:
- George T. Doran: There’s a S.M.A.R.T. Way to Write Management’s Goals and Objectives
- Locke and Latham: Building a Practically Useful Theory of Goal Setting
18. Q: Why is an action plan necessary even when a goal is SMART?
A: Show answer
A goal describes the result to be achieved; an action plan explains how the responsible people will pursue it. The plan should identify tasks, sequencing, owners, resources, dependencies, decision points, risks, and evidence of completion. Doran's original article stresses that the objective and its action plan work together; a well-written objective alone does not ensure implementation. The chapter's GE examples illustrate the distinction: a factory could write precise objectives, but performance changed only when workers examined processes, training, hiring, autonomy, and workflow. An action plan should be detailed enough to coordinate work while remaining open to revision as teams learn more about the task.
Study instruction:
- For one SMART objective, create a short action plan with owner, first step, dependency, resource, milestone, and review date.
Key point:
- The goal defines success; the plan organizes the route.
- Update the plan when evidence shows the route is not working.
Source links:
- George T. Doran: There’s a S.M.A.R.T. Way to Write Management’s Goals and Objectives
- Locke and Latham: Building a Practically Useful Theory of Goal Setting
19. Q: How should long-term goals, objectives, and short-term tasks relate to one another?
A: Show answer
A useful hierarchy links a broad purpose to measurable outcomes and then to the actions that can influence them. A long-term goal describes the direction or value to be created. Objectives make parts of that direction more concrete. Proximal milestones and tasks specify what can be done soon enough to guide daily behavior. The hierarchy should work in both directions: each short-term task should contribute to an objective, and each objective should still serve the broader purpose. If tasks are completed but the outcome does not improve, the team should examine whether the intermediate objective or the strategy is wrong. This prevents a calendar full of completed tasks from being mistaken for strategic progress.
Study instruction:
- Draw a three-level map from one strategic ambition to two measurable objectives and the next practical actions supporting them.
Key point:
- Short-term activity should remain connected to long-term purpose.
- Recheck the chain when completed tasks do not produce results.
Source links:
- George T. Doran: There’s a S.M.A.R.T. Way to Write Management’s Goals and Objectives
- undefined
20. Q: Why do specific and challenging goals often improve performance compared with vague or easy goals?
A: Show answer
Goal-setting research finds that specific, challenging goals can improve performance by directing attention, mobilizing effort, encouraging persistence, and prompting people to use more effective strategies. A vague instruction such as “do your best” does not create a clear standard for choosing actions or evaluating progress. Difficulty matters because an undemanding target may not require people to use their full capability. However, the effect depends on conditions such as task complexity, ability, commitment, feedback, and access to strategies. Difficult goals are not automatically superior in every setting, especially where the task is unfamiliar, interdependent, or safety-critical. The chapter uses SMART goals to make challenge actionable rather than merely exhortational.
Study instruction:
- Compare a vague target with a specific challenging target and identify the changes each would make to attention, effort, and feedback.
Key point:
- Specific challenge can focus effort and strategy.
- Calibrate difficulty to task, capability, and commitment.
Source links:
- Locke and Latham: Building a Practically Useful Theory of Goal Setting
21. Q: What did the typists' experiment in the chapter demonstrate about specific goals?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes a study in which experienced typists first had their output measured, then worked with researchers to set individual performance targets, establish a way to measure progress, and agree on a realistic timeline. Their average output rose from the initial baseline over the following weeks and remained higher when performance was checked later. The important lesson is not that a brief conversation guarantees the same increase in every workplace. It is that people can improve when a goal is made concrete, progress becomes visible, and the target is calibrated with the person doing the work. The process also helps reveal practical changes needed to reach the target rather than relying on pressure alone.
Study instruction:
- Identify the baseline, target-setting discussion, measurement method, and follow-up in the typists' example; explain what each contributed.
Key point:
- Specific goals become more actionable when paired with measurement and feedback.
- A case example should not be treated as a universal performance guarantee.
Source links:
- Locke and Latham: Building a Practically Useful Theory of Goal Setting
22. Q: Why is feedback essential to effective goal setting?
A: Show answer
Feedback tells people whether their actions are producing the intended result and whether the plan needs adjustment. Without it, a goal can remain a statement of intent rather than a learning process. Useful feedback is timely, credible, connected to the target, and specific enough to guide a next action. It should distinguish effort from results and explain whether a gap reflects capability, strategy, resources, measurement error, or changing conditions. Feedback also helps people recognize progress, sustain commitment, and detect unintended consequences. In complex work, feedback may need to be qualitative and diagnostic rather than a single score. The chapter's examples show why a measurement system should help people understand performance, not merely report it.
Study instruction:
- Design a feedback loop for one goal: data source, review frequency, responsible reviewer, decision triggered by a variance, and record of the response.
Key point:
- Goals need feedback to guide correction and learning.
- A metric without interpretation is not a complete feedback system.
Source links:
- Locke and Latham: Building a Practically Useful Theory of Goal Setting
23. Q: What is goal commitment, and how can it be strengthened?
A: Show answer
Goal commitment is the willingness to persist toward a goal rather than abandon it when obstacles arise. It is influenced by whether people regard the target as meaningful, whether they believe progress is possible, and whether they have had a credible role in understanding or accepting the objective. Participation can reveal practical constraints and improve ownership, although participation does not mean every target must be negotiated or that agreement guarantees success. Commitment should be supported by clear rationale, resources, feedback, and fair accountability. Managers should also distinguish genuine commitment from public compliance: a person may say yes while privately believing the goal is impossible or unsafe to pursue.
Study instruction:
- Ask a team member what makes a goal worthwhile, what could prevent progress, and what support would make commitment credible.
Key point:
- Commitment depends on meaning, confidence, and conditions for action.
- Agreement should be tested through practical readiness, not assumed from silence.
Source links:
- Locke and Latham: Building a Practically Useful Theory of Goal Setting
- See, Heath, and Fox: Motivating Individual Performance with Challenging Goals
24. Q: How does task complexity affect the way goals should be set?
A: Show answer
A simple, familiar task may respond well to a clear performance target because the person already understands the method. A complex or unfamiliar task may require learning goals, experimentation, intermediate milestones, and time to develop a strategy before a demanding outcome target is useful. If a team is still discovering how a process works, insisting on a precise short-term result can encourage premature shortcuts or conceal uncertainty. The goal system should therefore reflect what is known about the task. It may begin with learning and capability milestones, then move toward more exact performance measures as the team gains experience and reliable feedback.
Study instruction:
- Classify a current task as familiar or complex and decide whether its first target should emphasize performance, learning, or both.
Key point:
- Goal design should fit the task's complexity and the team's knowledge.
- Learning milestones may be necessary before outcome targets can be calibrated.
Source links:
- Locke and Latham: Building a Practically Useful Theory of Goal Setting
25. Q: How can SMART goals create tunnel vision?
A: Show answer
A highly structured goal can narrow attention to the measures, deadlines, and activities that have been formally specified. People may focus on satisfying the target even when conditions change or when the target no longer serves the broader purpose. The chapter describes GE employees who spent substantial effort writing detailed goals for low-value tasks, and it explains how Zeira's desire for clear estimates narrowed attention to evidence that supported his concept. Tunnel vision is more likely when performance is judged through a small set of indicators, dissent is discouraged, or success is defined as completing the plan rather than achieving the mission. A review process should therefore examine both execution and relevance.
Study instruction:
- For one active target, ask what important outcome is not measured and what change would make the target obsolete.
Key point:
- Precision can improve execution while narrowing attention.
- Review whether the goal remains worth achieving.
Source links:
- Locke and Latham: Building a Practically Useful Theory of Goal Setting
- Kruglanski and Webster: Motivated Closing of the Mind
26. Q: What was wrong with the trivial SMART goals found in GE's factories?
A: Show answer
The problem was not that the goals lacked detail. Many were specific, measurable, feasible, and time-bound, but they addressed minor tasks rather than the factory's most consequential performance problems. Employees could receive satisfaction from completing a precise objective while the division's profits, quality, or productivity continued to deteriorate. This is a form of goal displacement: the means of managing work become more important than the purpose of the work. A goal review should therefore ask whether the target contributes materially to strategic outcomes, whether the expected benefit justifies its cost, and what more important work is being crowded out by pursuing it.
Study instruction:
- Select three current objectives and rank them by expected contribution to the organization's mission, not by how easy they are to measure.
Key point:
- A SMART goal can be precisely wrong.
- Strategic relevance must be tested before administrative completeness.
Source links:
- Locke and Latham: Building a Practically Useful Theory of Goal Setting
- George T. Doran: There’s a S.M.A.R.T. Way to Write Management’s Goals and Objectives
27. Q: What is “mood repair” in the chapter's discussion of to-do lists?
A: Show answer
Mood repair occurs when a person chooses small, easy tasks mainly because crossing them off creates an immediate feeling of accomplishment or relief. The person may appear busy while postponing work that is more important, uncertain, or demanding. The chapter uses this behavior to explain why a list can become a source of emotional reassurance rather than a tool for achieving meaningful goals. The corrective approach is not to remove all small tasks; routine tasks still matter. It is to place them in the context of a larger ambition, prioritize by contribution and consequence, and reserve focused time for work that advances the important outcome.
Study instruction:
- Review yesterday's completed tasks and identify which ones advanced a major goal versus which ones mainly produced quick completion.
Key point:
- Feeling productive is not proof of meaningful progress.
- Prioritize by value and contribution, not only by ease of completion.
Source links:
- Locke and Latham: Building a Practically Useful Theory of Goal Setting
- undefined
28. Q: How can a to-do list keep daily tasks connected to a stretch ambition?
A: Show answer
A useful list places the larger ambition where it remains visible and then translates it into the next concrete actions. The chapter describes Timothy Pychyl writing a stretch objective at the top of his list and placing specific short-term tasks underneath, such as downloading an application by the following day. This structure gives daily work both direction and immediacy: the larger ambition explains why the tasks matter, while the proximal actions explain what to do next. The list should be reviewed so that tasks do not accumulate merely because they are easy to record. Items that no longer support the ambition should be removed, delegated, or reconsidered.
Study instruction:
- Put one important long-term aim at the top of a weekly list and write three next actions with owners or dates beneath it.
Key point:
- Keep the ambition visible while making the next step concrete.
- Remove activity that no longer contributes to the desired outcome.
Source links:
- undefined
- Locke and Latham: Building a Practically Useful Theory of Goal Setting
29. Q: What were GE's Work-Out meetings designed to achieve?
A: Show answer
Work-Out meetings were designed to help employees identify larger ambitions and propose changes without first forcing every idea into a fully specified SMART format. The chapter describes them as a way to counter a culture in which workers had become skilled at writing achievable objectives but were not thinking expansively about the factory's underlying problems. Employees could suggest goals they believed the organization should pursue, and managers were expected to respond quickly. Once an idea was accepted for exploration, the team could work out the practical details, feasibility, measures, and timing. The process separated the creative task of identifying worthwhile opportunities from the later task of planning implementation.
Study instruction:
- Plan a short idea-generation session for a process problem and specify how proposals will be assessed after the session.
Key point:
- Create room for ambition before narrowing ideas into plans.
- Follow exploration with disciplined evaluation and execution.
Source links:
- Locke and Latham: Building a Practically Useful Theory of Goal Setting
- George T. Doran: There’s a S.M.A.R.T. Way to Write Management’s Goals and Objectives
30. Q: Why did GE's Work-Out process delay detailed SMART analysis until after an idea was accepted for exploration?
A: Show answer
Requiring a complete business case too early can cause people to reject promising ideas before they have investigated them. Front-loading every feasibility test may favor familiar, incremental proposals because those are easiest to explain. Work-Outs encouraged employees to identify an important ambition first, then allowed managers to authorize a trial or further exploration. The subsequent process could determine whether the idea was workable, how success would be measured, and what resources were needed. This does not mean accepting every proposal or ignoring risk. It means sequencing creativity and control so that early uncertainty does not automatically suppress potentially valuable innovation.
Study instruction:
- Separate an idea's potential value from its current level of detail; identify what evidence a small pilot could generate before full approval.
Key point:
- Do not demand implementation-level certainty at the earliest idea stage.
- Use staged evaluation to manage uncertainty without extinguishing initiative.
Source links:
- Sitkin et al.: The Paradox of Stretch Goals
- Locke and Latham: Building a Practically Useful Theory of Goal Setting
31. Q: How can employee participation improve the quality of goal setting?
A: Show answer
Employees often understand operational constraints and improvement opportunities that are not visible to senior managers. Inviting them to propose goals can surface practical knowledge, build ownership, and reveal changes that a top-down target might miss. The chapter's GE examples show workers identifying a lower-cost way to produce machine shields and proposing changes to how work was organized. Participation is most useful when leaders listen seriously, explain decisions, provide a fair route for testing ideas, and make clear who has authority to approve resources. It should not become a substitute for accountability or technical review. The objective is to combine local knowledge with organizational direction and sound evaluation.
Study instruction:
- Ask frontline staff to identify one recurring obstacle and one improvement opportunity; document how management will test or respond to each proposal.
Key point:
- Frontline knowledge can expose opportunities missed by formal planning.
- Participation works when proposals receive a credible response.
Source links:
- Locke and Latham: Building a Practically Useful Theory of Goal Setting
- George T. Doran: There’s a S.M.A.R.T. Way to Write Management’s Goals and Objectives
32. Q: What were the limitations of GE's Work-Out meetings?
A: Show answer
Work-Outs required significant time and could slow production while employees attended. They were therefore difficult to hold frequently, and the excitement generated by a single meeting could fade when people returned to familiar routines. The chapter describes the need to create a more continuous culture of expansive thinking rather than relying only on occasional events. Organizations can address this by building idea review into regular management routines, assigning owners to experiments, protecting time for improvement, and tracking whether accepted ideas are implemented. A meeting is not an outcome. Its value depends on whether proposals become tested changes, whether learning is shared, and whether the organization continues to question established practices.
Study instruction:
- For an improvement workshop, define how ideas will be tracked, who will own pilots, and when the organization will review results.
Key point:
- Inspiration fades without ownership and follow-through.
- Embed learning and improvement into normal operating routines.
Source links:
- Locke and Latham: Building a Practically Useful Theory of Goal Setting
33. Q: What is a stretch goal?
A: Show answer
A stretch goal is an unusually ambitious objective that pushes beyond the organization's current assumptions about what can be achieved. In the chapter's description of Jack Welch's approach, a stretch target is so demanding that managers may not initially know how to reach it. Its purpose is to challenge settled thinking, reveal constraints, and encourage exploration of new methods rather than merely increase effort within the existing process. A stretch goal is not simply a larger numerical target. It should create a reason to reconsider systems, skills, structures, or products. Because it may exceed current capability, it must be paired with learning, experimentation, resources, and realistic intermediate plans.
Study instruction:
- Identify one area where incremental improvement is insufficient and write a stretch ambition that would require a different approach, not just more effort.
Key point:
- Stretch goals challenge assumptions and invite new methods.
- Ambition needs a credible learning and implementation process.
Source links:
- Sitkin et al.: The Paradox of Stretch Goals
- Locke and Latham: Building a Practically Useful Theory of Goal Setting
34. Q: What does the Shinkansen story illustrate about ambitious goals and innovation?
A: Show answer
The chapter uses the development of Japan's Tōkaidō Shinkansen to illustrate how an ambitious target can prompt engineers to question constraints that initially seem unavoidable. Rather than accepting incremental speed improvements, the railway challenge encouraged redesign across trains, tracks, tunnels, and related systems. The official Central Japan Railway history confirms that the Tōkaidō Shinkansen began service in 1964 and describes its role in connecting major metropolitan areas. The lesson is not that every ambitious target will produce a breakthrough. It is that a sufficiently meaningful challenge can cause people to examine the whole system rather than optimize one component while leaving the underlying limitations untouched.
Study instruction:
- Choose a process that has improved only incrementally and list the system assumptions that would need to change to achieve a step-change result.
Key point:
- Ambitious goals can trigger system-level redesign.
- Innovation may require changing several interdependent components.
Source links:
- Central Japan Railway: About the Shinkansen
- Sitkin et al.: The Paradox of Stretch Goals
35. Q: How did GE's 70 percent defect-reduction target change the aircraft-engine division's approach?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes managers who initially expected a smaller reduction to be achievable through additional inspection. When the target was raised to 70 percent, they recognized that simply adding inspectors would not address the scale of the problem. They examined error patterns, retrained employees, changed hiring criteria, expanded worker responsibility for quality, and gave teams more autonomy over scheduling and workflow. The goal therefore prompted changes to the operating system rather than a narrow increase in checking. The case illustrates how a stretch target can expose the inadequacy of the existing method. It does not establish that a 70 percent target is appropriate for every production environment.
Study instruction:
- Trace the chain from the ambitious target to the changes in training, recruitment, work design, and quality responsibility.
Key point:
- A stretch target can reveal that the process itself must change.
- Do not assume more inspection alone will solve systemic defects.
Source links:
- Sitkin et al.: The Paradox of Stretch Goals
- Locke and Latham: Building a Practically Useful Theory of Goal Setting
36. Q: Through what mechanisms can stretch goals encourage innovation?
A: Show answer
A stretch goal can disrupt complacency by making the current way of working visibly inadequate. It may encourage broader search, experimentation, collaboration across roles, and reconsideration of technologies or structures that would not be examined under a modest target. It can also create a shared challenge that gives people permission to question established routines. These mechanisms depend on the environment: people need enough skill, information, psychological safety, time, and resources to explore alternatives. If the organization responds to failure only with blame, the same ambition may suppress experimentation. Stretch goals are therefore most useful when leaders support learning and treat early setbacks as information rather than proof that employees are unwilling to work hard.
Study instruction:
- For a proposed stretch goal, identify two existing assumptions to challenge and one safe experiment that could test a new approach.
Key point:
- Innovation comes from exploration and system change, not pressure alone.
- Support experimentation with capability and learning conditions.
Source links:
- Sitkin et al.: The Paradox of Stretch Goals
- Locke and Latham: Building a Practically Useful Theory of Goal Setting
37. Q: What are the principal risks of setting a stretch goal that is far beyond reach?
A: Show answer
An extreme target can make success appear impossible, weaken commitment, increase anxiety, encourage risky shortcuts, and demoralize people who see little connection between effort and progress. It can also distort reporting if staff feel compelled to hide setbacks or manipulate measures. The research discussed in the chapter cautions that ambitious goals can produce innovation but can also produce panic. See, Heath, and Fox's experimental work found that a goal much farther away did not motivate all participants as well as a less distant challenging goal. At organizational level, Sitkin and colleagues emphasize that the risks of stretch goals depend on the organization's circumstances. Leaders should assess capability, prior performance, resources, and the likely cost of failure.
Study instruction:
- Conduct a stretch-goal risk review covering commitment, capability, resource slack, safety, incentives, and possible reporting distortions.
Key point:
- Extreme ambition can demotivate as well as energize.
- Evaluate who bears the risk and what happens if the target is missed.
Source links:
- Sitkin et al.: The Paradox of Stretch Goals
- See, Heath, and Fox: Motivating Individual Performance with Challenging Goals
38. Q: When should an organization avoid imposing a uniform stretch goal on everyone?
A: Show answer
A uniform stretch goal may be inappropriate when people have substantially different starting capabilities, resources, roles, or control over the outcome. A target that stretches a high performer may be unattainable and demoralizing for someone who lacks the necessary experience or support. The chapter's discussion of the Duke study and related research warns against assuming that one extreme target will motivate an entire distribution of performers equally. Organizations should examine baseline performance, task difficulty, dependencies, and the effect of the target on safety and quality. Differentiated goals or staged targets may be fairer and more productive than a single headline number applied without regard to context.
Study instruction:
- Compare team members' starting conditions and decide whether one target, tiered targets, or individual milestones would better support performance.
Key point:
- One target can have different effects on people with different capabilities.
- Calibrate challenge rather than equating fairness with identical numbers.
Source links:
- See, Heath, and Fox: Motivating Individual Performance with Challenging Goals
- Sitkin et al.: The Paradox of Stretch Goals
39. Q: Why does the chapter recommend combining stretch goals with SMART thinking?
A: Show answer
Stretch goals provide direction and challenge, while SMART thinking turns the ambition into concrete next steps. A large ambition alone can be intimidating because people may not know where to begin. A series of short-term objectives makes progress visible and helps people identify the capabilities, resources, and decisions required. Conversely, SMART goals without a stretch ambition can encourage people to focus on easy, measurable tasks that do not advance important change. The two approaches work as complements: one asks what worthwhile future should be pursued, and the other asks what practical action can be taken next. The organization must still review whether the ambition remains appropriate as circumstances evolve.
Study instruction:
- Pair one ambitious outcome with three near-term objectives, each with a measure, owner, deadline, and learning checkpoint.
Key point:
- Stretch provides direction; SMART planning provides traction.
- Keep short-term tasks connected to the larger ambition.
Source links:
- Locke and Latham: Building a Practically Useful Theory of Goal Setting
- Sitkin et al.: The Paradox of Stretch Goals
40. Q: What are proximal goals, and how do they help people pursue a distant ambition?
A: Show answer
Proximal goals are nearer-term subgoals that divide a distant objective into manageable steps. They help people see what to do now, monitor progress, and build a sense of competence as they master successive tasks. The chapter uses running and business examples to show how a large ambition becomes more actionable when translated into progress over a day, week, or month. Research by Bandura and Schunk examined proximal subgoals in self-directed learning and found benefits for progress, perceived efficacy, and intrinsic interest in the studied context. Proximal goals should be meaningful stepping stones, not a collection of unrelated small tasks that merely produce a feeling of completion.
Study instruction:
- Break one distant goal into three milestones and write the first observable action that can be completed this week.
Key point:
- Proximal goals make distant aims actionable and progress visible.
- Each subgoal should contribute to the larger outcome.
Source links:
- undefined
- Locke and Latham: Building a Practically Useful Theory of Goal Setting
41. Q: What did the Duke runners experiment compare?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes varsity runners asked to move as close as possible to a finish line during a final ten-second interval. In the underlying manuscript by See, Heath, and Fox, the runners completed two conditions on a 400-meter track: in one, the finish line was 100 meters away when the signal sounded; in the other, it was 200 meters away. The researchers used a within-person design so that each runner experienced both conditions on separate occasions. The study was designed to examine how progress toward a goal can affect effort even when the goal is known to be unattainable within the remaining time. It should be understood as a specific experiment, not a universal rule about all stretch targets.
Study instruction:
- Explain the two conditions and why using the same runners in both conditions helped control individual differences.
Key point:
- The experiment compared a nearer and a much farther goal under controlled conditions.
- Interpret the findings within the study's sample and task.
Source links:
- See, Heath, and Fox: Motivating Individual Performance with Challenging Goals
42. Q: What result did the Duke runners experiment report, and what does it suggest?
A: Show answer
In the final ten seconds, participants ran an average of 63.1 meters in the condition where the finish line was 100 meters away, compared with 59.6 meters when it was 200 meters away. The manuscript reports that the difference was statistically significant in the study sample. The authors interpreted the result as evidence that a goal closer to reach can prompt greater effort than a goal far beyond reach, even when participants know neither target can be attained in the available time. The result does not prove that easier goals always outperform harder goals. It illustrates that the psychological distance to a target can matter and that extremely distant targets may weaken the motivational effect for some performers.
Study instruction:
- Record the two reported averages and state one conclusion the study supports and one conclusion it does not justify.
Key point:
- A nearer challenging goal can produce more effort than an extreme distant goal in some conditions.
- Avoid turning a single experiment into a universal prescription.
Source links:
- See, Heath, and Fox: Motivating Individual Performance with Challenging Goals
43. Q: How should managers calibrate the difficulty of a challenging goal?
A: Show answer
Managers should consider the individual's or team's baseline performance, relevant skill, task complexity, available resources, and confidence in the proposed route. They should also examine whether the goal can be meaningfully approached through effort and strategy, or whether it is so distant that people cannot identify useful intermediate progress. A target may be adjusted as evidence accumulates, and different levels of challenge may be appropriate for different capability levels. Calibration is not an excuse to remove ambition; it is a way to keep challenge connected to learning and performance. Where the objective is genuinely exploratory, managers can set learning milestones rather than pretending that the final result is predictable.
Study instruction:
- Use baseline data and a capability assessment to justify why a proposed target is demanding but still actionable.
Key point:
- Challenge should stretch capability without destroying a sense of agency.
- Use evidence and milestones to recalibrate targets.
Source links:
- See, Heath, and Fox: Motivating Individual Performance with Challenging Goals
- Sitkin et al.: The Paradox of Stretch Goals
44. Q: How can tiered or individualized goals reduce the risks of a uniform stretch target?
A: Show answer
Tiered goals provide different levels of challenge while preserving a shared direction. A baseline target can define expected progress for most participants, while a higher stretch level can challenge those with greater capability or additional resources. Individualized goals can reflect starting points, role differences, or constraints that a single number would ignore. The approach should be transparent and based on relevant evidence, not favoritism. It should also avoid labeling people permanently by current performance; capability can change through training and experience. The chapter's discussion of the runners study suggests that an extreme common goal may help some high performers while reducing the effort of others, making calibration an important management decision.
Study instruction:
- Design two or three goal levels for a mixed-capability team and define the evidence used to place or move people between levels.
Key point:
- Differentiated goals can balance challenge and attainability.
- Review placement as skills and conditions change.
Source links:
- See, Heath, and Fox: Motivating Individual Performance with Challenging Goals
- Sitkin et al.: The Paradox of Stretch Goals
45. Q: How should progress toward a stretch goal be monitored without reducing it to a narrow metric?
A: Show answer
Monitoring should combine outcome measures with indicators of learning, capability, quality, safety, and unintended effects. A stretch goal may not have a clear route at the outset, so the organization should track whether experiments are producing useful information and whether constraints are being removed. Managers should review both leading indicators, which suggest whether progress is possible, and lagging results, which show what has already happened. They should also ask whether the chosen measures still represent the intended outcome. If the metric improves while quality or mission performance deteriorates, the team should not declare success. Monitoring should support adaptation rather than enforce blind adherence to the original plan.
Study instruction:
- Build a review dashboard with an outcome measure, two leading indicators, one quality safeguard, and a field for decisions arising from exceptions.
Key point:
- Monitor results and the health of the process producing them.
- Use measures to learn, not to silence contrary evidence.
Source links:
- Locke and Latham: Building a Practically Useful Theory of Goal Setting
- Sitkin et al.: The Paradox of Stretch Goals
46. Q: When should a goal be revised or abandoned?
A: Show answer
A goal should be reviewed when its assumptions no longer hold, the environment changes materially, evidence shows that the chosen route is ineffective, or the target creates unacceptable harm or risk. Revision may mean changing the method, resources, timeline, measure, or target itself. Abandonment may be appropriate when the objective no longer supports the mission or when pursuing it would be irresponsible. A review should distinguish ordinary difficulty from evidence that the premise is wrong. Teams should document why the goal changed, who approved the change, what commitments are affected, and what learning should be retained. Revising a goal is not necessarily failure; refusing to revise a flawed goal can be the greater failure.
Study instruction:
- Define three review triggers for a current goal and identify who has authority to approve a change or stop the work.
Key point:
- Commitment should not become rigidity.
- Change course when evidence, mission, or risk requires it.
Source links:
- Kruglanski and Webster: Motivated Closing of the Mind
- Locke and Latham: Building a Practically Useful Theory of Goal Setting
47. Q: How can the chapter's goal-setting principles be applied to an NGO or project team?
A: Show answer
An NGO project team can begin with a meaningful outcome for participants or communities, then translate it into measurable objectives and near-term milestones. A target such as improving access to a service should specify the population, service standard, timeframe, and evidence of change. The team should involve frontline staff and partners in identifying barriers, because they may know constraints that headquarters cannot see. Stretch ambition can encourage new delivery models, while SMART planning clarifies budgets, responsibilities, data collection, and review dates. The team must also protect safeguarding, quality, inclusion, and donor or legal obligations. A numerical target must never encourage staff to compromise consent, safety, or the actual needs of participants.
Study instruction:
- Turn one project ambition into an outcome objective, a near-term milestone, an accountable owner, and one quality or safeguarding safeguard.
Key point:
- Align project targets with participant outcomes and operational safeguards.
- Involve people closest to delivery when designing the plan.
Source links:
- Locke and Latham: Building a Practically Useful Theory of Goal Setting
- George T. Doran: There’s a S.M.A.R.T. Way to Write Management’s Goals and Objectives
48. Q: What does the Yom Kippur War case teach about the relationship between goals and organizational purpose?
A: Show answer
The chapter shows how an operational goal can become detached from the mission it was meant to serve. Zeira wanted clearer estimates and fewer unnecessary alarms, but the desire for decisive, reassuring conclusions helped suppress warnings that should have triggered further examination. The Israel Ministry of Defense Archives describes the surprise attack on 6 October 1973 and the establishment of the Agranat Commission to examine intelligence available before the war, its analysis, and military preparedness. The lesson is not to abandon clear objectives; it is to test whether local measures still serve the higher purpose. A goal system must include escalation, dissent, alternative scenarios, and review when new indicators appear.
Study instruction:
- Map one operational target to its higher-level mission and list the warning signs that would show the target is undermining that mission.
Key point:
- Local efficiency can conflict with the purpose it is meant to support.
- Revisit the mission when evidence challenges the plan.
Source links:
- Israel Ministry of Defense Archives: Agranat Committee
- Israel Ministry of Defense Archives: The Agranat Commission Report
49. Q: What is the value of Zeira's reminder to ask “and if not?”
A: Show answer
The question “and if not?” is a prompt to consider alternatives rather than treating the preferred explanation as certain. It encourages decision makers to ask what would happen if an assumption is false, what other explanation fits the evidence, and what preparations would be needed under that alternative. The question is useful before a decision and during implementation, especially when the cost of being wrong is high. It should lead to concrete analysis: identify plausible alternatives, indicators that distinguish them, and actions that preserve options. It is not a reason to imagine every remote possibility or paralyze action. Its purpose is to prevent confidence from eliminating contingency thinking.
Study instruction:
- For a consequential decision, write the preferred forecast, one credible alternative, the evidence that would distinguish them, and a response for each.
Key point:
- Ask what would change your mind before the pressure to defend a decision grows.
- Contingency thinking complements decisive action.
Source links:
- Kruglanski and Webster: Motivated Closing of the Mind
- Israel Ministry of Defense Archives: Agranat Committee
50. Q: What integrated goal-setting cycle best captures the chapter's practical message?
A: Show answer
Begin by defining the higher-level purpose and selecting an ambition that matters. Invite people with relevant knowledge to identify opportunities and constraints before requiring a complete plan. Translate the chosen ambition into specific objectives, measures, owners, resources, and timelines. Break distant outcomes into proximal actions that people can execute and review. Use feedback to assess both results and the assumptions behind the plan. Where progress is weak, investigate whether the problem lies in capability, strategy, resources, measurement, or the goal itself. Encourage dissent and ask “and if not?” so that commitment does not become rigidity. Finally, retain the willingness to revise or stop a goal that no longer serves the mission.
Study instruction:
- Apply the cycle to one real objective and document the purpose, ambition, action plan, milestones, feedback loop, alternative scenario, and revision trigger.
Key point:
- Strong goals combine ambition, executable steps, feedback, and openness to correction.
- The right goal matters more than the satisfaction of completing a plan.
Source links:
- Locke and Latham: Building a Practically Useful Theory of Goal Setting
- Sitkin et al.: The Paradox of Stretch Goals
- Kruglanski and Webster: Motivated Closing of the Mind

Table 5
Managing Others: Lean Thinking, Agile Teams, and a Culture of Trust
1. Q: What is the central lesson of Chapter 5, “Managing Others: Solving a Kidnapping with Lean and Agile Thinking and a Culture of Trust”?
A: Show answer
The chapter argues that people and organizations perform better when decision-making authority is placed close to the work, employees are encouraged to surface problems, and managers demonstrate that they are committed to the people doing the work. Lean manufacturing and Agile software development provide methods for making work visible, testing solutions quickly, and improving processes through collaboration. Those methods depend on trust: employees must believe that raising a problem, making a suggestion, or admitting a mistake will not lead to punishment. The NUMMI factory, the FBI’s Sentinel project, and the Frank Janssen investigation illustrate different applications of the same principle. Technology and process design matter, but their value depends on a culture that allows people to use their knowledge and take responsibility.
Study instruction:
- Compare the NUMMI, Sentinel, and Janssen examples and identify the common links between authority, trust, learning, and performance.
Key point:
- Empowerment works when people have both authority and confidence that others will support responsible action.
- Lean and Agile methods are strengthened by a culture of trust and shared responsibility.
Source links:
- Penguin Random House: Smarter Faster Better
- Amy Edmondson: Psychological Safety and Learning Behavior in Work Teams
2. Q: Why does the chapter begin with the kidnapping of Frank Janssen?
A: Show answer
The kidnapping story introduces a complex investigation in which police and federal agents collected hundreds of clues but initially struggled to connect them. The chapter describes confusing text messages, burner phones, uncertain references, prison contacts, and several competing explanations for the abduction. Investigators had information but lacked a way to see how separate pieces might relate. The story creates a practical example of a broader management problem: organizations often have capable people and large amounts of information, yet their systems and decision processes make it difficult to recognize patterns and act on them. The chapter later shows how the FBI’s Sentinel system and a more decentralized approach to decision-making helped investigators pursue a lead that had previously seemed unrelated.
Study instruction:
- List the main clues in the early investigation and explain why having many separate facts did not immediately produce a useful explanation.
Key point:
- Information becomes useful when people and systems can connect it to a decision.
- Complex problems require both analytical tools and empowered investigators.
Source links:
- Penguin Random House: Smarter Faster Better
- FBI: Information Sharing and Safeguarding Report
3. Q: Why were the messages sent by the kidnappers difficult for investigators to interpret?
A: Show answer
The messages referred to people and objects that investigators did not initially understand, including “Jefe,” “Franno,” “his things,” and demands involving the imprisoned gang leader Kelvin Melton. Some threats appeared inconsistent with the theory that the abduction was simply revenge against the prosecutor who had convicted Melton. Investigators also did not know why the kidnappers had not made a conventional ransom demand. These ambiguities made it difficult to decide which facts were connected and which were distractions. The chapter uses the case to show how a team can become overwhelmed when evidence is fragmented across phones, interviews, locations, and databases. A strong investigation requires structured records, competing hypotheses, and a way for people to follow up on promising leads.
Study instruction:
- Separate the messages into known facts, unexplained references, and assumptions, then identify what additional evidence would test each possible explanation.
Key point:
- Ambiguous information should be organized and tested rather than forced into one early theory.
- Investigative systems need to preserve links among clues without treating every coincidence as proof.
Source links:
- Penguin Random House: Smarter Faster Better
- FBI: Sentinel Privacy Impact Assessment
4. Q: What was Sentinel, and what problem was it designed to solve?
A: Show answer
Sentinel was the FBI’s electronic case-management system for organizing investigative records, evidence, leads, documents, and other information. It was intended to reduce dependence on paper files and outdated databases while improving search, workflow, and the ability to identify relationships across cases. The chapter describes earlier technology projects that had consumed large sums without delivering useful tools, leaving agents to rely on paper folders and index cards. Sentinel was designed to help investigators retrieve information and connect details that might otherwise remain separated. The FBI’s official privacy assessment describes Sentinel as a case-management system with document workflow, search, reporting, and analysis capabilities. It is important to distinguish this operational role from the chapter’s broader argument about the culture needed to use the system effectively.
Study instruction:
- Describe Sentinel’s main functions and distinguish information storage, case workflow, search, and analytical linking.
Key point:
- Sentinel was an electronic case-management and information-sharing system, not simply a stand-alone crime-prediction tool.
- Technology can improve access to evidence, but people still interpret and act on the results.
Source links:
- FBI: Sentinel Privacy Impact Assessment
- FBI: Information Sharing and Safeguarding Report
5. Q: Why did the FBI’s earlier technology projects fail to deliver the promised improvements?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes earlier FBI technology initiatives that were burdened by bureaucracy, conflicting agendas, extensive specifications, slow approval processes, and weak feedback from the people who would use the systems. Projects could spend months building software only to be canceled or redesigned after senior officials changed their requirements. The resulting systems were often too slow, difficult to use, or disconnected from agents’ daily needs. The underlying problem was not simply a lack of technical talent. The development process kept decision-making far from the users and required extensive planning before anyone could test whether the design solved a real problem. The chapter contrasts this with an approach that uses short development cycles, working prototypes, and regular feedback from users and developers.
Study instruction:
- Identify the process failures that made the earlier projects slow and compare them with the practices used by the later Sentinel team.
Key point:
- Excessive top-down specification can delay learning and disconnect software from users’ needs.
- Early testing and frequent feedback can reveal problems before they become expensive.
Source links:
- U.S. Department of Justice OIG: Interim Report on the FBI’s Sentinel Project
- Penguin Random House: Smarter Faster Better
6. Q: What did Chad Fulgham propose when he was asked to take over the Sentinel project?
A: Show answer
Chad Fulgham proposed applying lessons from lean manufacturing and Agile software development to the FBI’s technology work. Rather than rely on hundreds of people writing specifications and waiting for approvals, he wanted a smaller team of programmers and agents who could collaborate, build prototypes, and adjust the system as users identified problems. He argued that the people closest to a piece of code or an investigative need should have primary responsibility for solving it. The chapter reports that he offered to deliver the remaining Sentinel work with a much smaller team and a far lower incremental budget than the previous development effort. This proposal was about changing the development method and authority structure, not simply replacing one group of programmers with another.
Study instruction:
- Explain the differences between Fulgham’s proposed team structure and the earlier committee-driven approach.
Key point:
- Agile methods place more emphasis on collaboration, iteration, and user feedback.
- The people closest to a problem should have meaningful authority to solve it.
Source links:
- U.S. Department of Justice OIG: Sentinel Project Report
- FBI: Oversight of the Federal Bureau of Investigation
7. Q: What is lean manufacturing?
A: Show answer
Lean manufacturing is an approach to organizing production that seeks to reduce waste, improve quality, and make problems visible as close as possible to where they occur. The chapter describes Toyota’s system as one in which workers are encouraged to identify defects, stop the line when necessary, and help develop better tools and processes. Rather than allowing a mistake to move downstream and become more expensive to fix, the organization tries to detect and correct it at the source. Lean methods also emphasize learning from employees who understand the work in detail. Toyota’s own description of its production system highlights Just-in-Time and jidoka, often explained as automation with human judgment. Lean is therefore not simply a cost-cutting program; it combines process discipline with continuous improvement and respect for people’s knowledge.
Study instruction:
- Identify one form of waste in a work process and describe how a problem could be detected and corrected closer to its source.
Key point:
- Lean aims to improve flow and quality by exposing and addressing problems early.
- Continuous improvement depends on the knowledge of people doing the work.
Source links:
- Toyota: Basic Concept of the Toyota Production System
- Penguin Random House: Smarter Faster Better
8. Q: What was the main problem with the old GM Fremont factory before NUMMI reopened it?
A: Show answer
The chapter portrays the old Fremont plant as a workplace where management and labor distrusted one another and production speed mattered more than quality. Workers often allowed defects to continue down the assembly line because stopping the line was discouraged. Mistakes were repaired later, which required taking apart finished vehicles and could leave the product in worse condition. The chapter also describes absenteeism, substance abuse, sabotage, and a lack of meaningful employee input. Workers felt that their job was simply to keep the conveyor moving, not to take responsibility for the quality of the vehicle. The factory’s problems were therefore cultural and organizational as well as technical. The same workers later performed much better under a different management system and a more credible relationship with the employer.
Study instruction:
- Separate the factory’s problems into process design, management behavior, worker incentives, and trust, then identify how each affected quality.
Key point:
- Poor performance can reflect a broken work system rather than a lack of employee ability.
- Quality suffers when workers are discouraged from raising problems.
Source links:
- Penguin Random House: Smarter Faster Better
- Toyota: Basic Concept of the Toyota Production System
9. Q: Why did GM and Toyota create NUMMI, and what was each company trying to learn?
A: Show answer
NUMMI, or New United Motor Manufacturing, Inc., was a joint venture between General Motors and Toyota that reopened the Fremont plant in California. Toyota gained an opportunity to expand production in the United States, while GM wanted to learn how Toyota produced high-quality vehicles at relatively low cost. The partnership also had to work within an agreement requiring most workers to be rehired from the previous GM workforce. The chapter uses the arrangement to test a central question: could Toyota’s production methods and culture succeed with the same workers who had been part of a deeply dysfunctional factory? The result showed that performance could change dramatically when management altered the system, gave workers meaningful authority, and demonstrated commitment to their well-being.
Study instruction:
- Explain the different strategic interests of Toyota and GM and why retaining the previous workforce made the NUMMI experiment significant.
Key point:
- NUMMI tested whether a different management system could transform the performance of an existing workforce.
- Organizational context can unlock or suppress employee capability.
Source links:
- Penguin Random House: Smarter Faster Better
- Toyota: Basic Concept of the Toyota Production System
10. Q: What did Rick Madrid observe when a worker made a mistake on the Japanese assembly line?
A: Show answer
Madrid expected the worker to mark the defective vehicle and allow it to continue down the line, as had happened at the old Fremont plant. Instead, the worker pulled an overhead cord that activated a warning light, began correcting the problem, and caused the line to stop when the defect reached the end of the station. Managers arrived to help rather than punish him. The worker continued directing the repair and restarted the line when the problem was fixed. Madrid was surprised because he had been taught that the line must never stop, even when a serious defect had been found. The experience showed him that Toyota treated quality as a shared responsibility and accepted a short interruption to avoid a larger downstream failure.
Study instruction:
- Compare the old GM response to a defect with the Toyota response and identify the costs each approach creates later in the process.
Key point:
- Stopping work to fix a defect early can prevent larger quality failures later.
- Managers should support problem-solving rather than punish the person who identifies a problem.
Source links:
- Toyota: Basic Concept of the Toyota Production System
- Penguin Random House: Smarter Faster Better
11. Q: What is an andon cord, and why is it important in the chapter?
A: Show answer
An andon cord is a mechanism that allows a worker to signal a problem on the production line. In the chapter, pulling the cord activates a warning light and can stop the line so that a defect or process issue can be addressed before it moves further downstream. Its importance is not merely mechanical. The cord represents a transfer of authority: the worker who sees the problem can initiate action without waiting for a senior manager to notice or approve it. That authority is meaningful only if employees believe they will not be punished for using it responsibly. At NUMMI, workers initially hesitated because they did not know whether management would honor its promises. The president’s visible support helped turn the cord into a real tool for quality and accountability.
Study instruction:
- Identify an equivalent “andon cord” in your workplace: a formal way for any employee to pause work and escalate a serious quality, safety, or compliance concern.
Key point:
- An andon cord makes problems visible and gives frontline workers authority to act.
- Empowerment requires a credible assurance that responsible escalation will be supported.
Source links:
- Toyota: Basic Concept of the Toyota Production System
- Penguin Random House: Smarter Faster Better
12. Q: What does jidoka mean in the Toyota Production System?
A: Show answer
Toyota describes jidoka as a principle that can be translated as automation with human wisdom. It means that production equipment and workers should detect abnormalities and stop or correct a process when a problem occurs, rather than allowing defects to continue. The chapter’s andon-cord example shows how this principle works in practice: a worker notices a misthreaded bolt, signals the problem, and receives support to fix it before the vehicle moves on. Jidoka is therefore about quality at the source and the ability to respond intelligently when something goes wrong. It does not mean that every minor issue must stop all work indefinitely; it means that the process should make defects visible and support timely correction.
Study instruction:
- Explain how jidoka differs from a system that relies only on final inspection after production is complete.
Key point:
- Jidoka combines process automation with human judgment and problem detection.
- Quality should be built into the process, not left entirely to downstream inspection.
Source links:
- Toyota: Basic Concept of the Toyota Production System
13. Q: Why does lean manufacturing push decision-making authority toward the people closest to a problem?
A: Show answer
Frontline employees often see defects, delays, and awkward procedures before managers or executives do. They also understand the practical details of the work and may know which small changes would improve quality or reduce waste. If every suggestion must travel through several layers of management, useful information can be delayed, diluted, or ignored.
