- "Write a one-sentence central idea for a project and test whether its main components consistently support that idea."
Key point:
- A clear central conflict helps unify separate creative contributions.
- A core idea provides a criterion for evaluating story and design choices.
46. Q: What does the Frozen ending illustrate about reframing a familiar convention?
A: Show answer
The final story retained recognizable fairy-tale elements but changed their function. The prince was not the rescuer; Hans became the antagonist. The princesses were not passive recipients of rescue; Anna saved Elsa through an act of sacrifice, and the sisters ultimately supported one another. True love was expressed through the relationship between siblings rather than a romantic kiss. These changes disturbed familiar conventions just enough to create a different emotional meaning while keeping the story accessible. The example demonstrates that innovation does not require rejecting every traditional element. It can arise by changing who acts, what a convention means and how the pieces fit together.
Study instruction:
- "Choose a familiar genre convention and propose a version that preserves its audience-recognizable form while changing its underlying meaning."
Key point:
- Frozen transformed familiar fairy-tale conventions rather than discarding all of them.
- Changing the role or meaning of a convention can create a fresh experience.
47. Q: How can a leader introduce a useful disturbance without undermining team stability?
A: Show answer
A leader should identify the pattern that has become entrenched and choose the smallest intervention likely to create a new perspective. Examples include rotating who leads a review, inviting a colleague from another function, asking an outsider to challenge the assumptions, or giving a capable contributor temporary decision authority. The change should have a clear purpose, a defined scope and a way to evaluate its effects. Leaders should protect essential responsibilities and explain that the intervention is intended to improve the work, not punish the team. The ecological analogy warns against both ineffective minor adjustments and destabilizing upheaval.
Study instruction:
- "Design a low-risk intervention for a team that is stuck, including its objective, scope, duration and evaluation criteria."
Key point:
- Effective disruption is deliberate and proportionate.
- A useful change creates alternatives without destroying the team's ability to function.
48. Q: Why can tension among collaborators improve divergent thinking?
A: Show answer
The chapter explains that differences in background, opinion and perspective can force people to look at an idea from another person's point of view. This can produce divergent thinking: the ability to see possibilities that would not emerge if everyone approached the problem in the same way. Tension is productive when it challenges assumptions while preserving enough respect and trust for people to exchange ideas. If conflict becomes personal or overwhelming, it can suppress rather than expand thinking. The goal is therefore not constant disagreement. It is to maintain a constructive level of difference that prevents premature consensus and keeps alternatives visible.
Study instruction:
- "Assess whether disagreement in a team is generating new options or merely producing defensiveness, and identify what would make it more constructive."
Key point:
- Differences can trigger new perspectives and divergent thinking.
- Tension must remain constructive to support rather than suppress creativity.
49. Q: How can a team avoid premature consensus after finding a promising solution?
A: Show answer
A team can protect against premature consensus by scheduling a deliberate challenge phase after an apparent breakthrough. Members can ask what evidence would disconfirm the idea, what alternatives were rejected too quickly, and how the solution would perform under different conditions. A reviewer who was not involved in creating the solution can provide critical distance. The team should also preserve a record of viable alternatives until the central assumptions have been tested. The Frozen example shows that relief after solving major story problems made the team less flexible. A formal review can counter that tendency before commitment becomes too difficult to reverse.
Study instruction:
- "Create a post-breakthrough review with questions about disconfirming evidence, alternative options, user needs and untested assumptions."
Key point:
- A promising solution should still be challenged before it becomes fixed.
- Independent review helps prevent relief from turning into premature certainty.
50. Q: How can an organization create more opportunities for idea brokerage?
A: Show answer
Organizations can create brokerage opportunities by connecting people across departments, disciplines, locations and levels of seniority. Cross-functional projects, job shadowing, rotating review groups, shared learning sessions and open demonstrations of work can expose employees to methods they would not otherwise encounter. Leaders should reward useful knowledge transfer rather than treating expertise as something to be guarded within a team. They should also give brokers access to the specialists who can assess technical feasibility. The aim is to make ideas mobile while retaining the expertise and accountability needed to adapt them responsibly.
Study instruction:
- "Choose two organizational boundaries that restrict knowledge exchange and propose a recurring activity that connects the people on either side."
Key point:
- Brokerage depends on real connections across otherwise separate groups.
- Organizations can design work to make knowledge exchange more likely.
51. Q: What practical steps can an individual take to become a more effective innovation broker?
A: Show answer
An individual can begin by collecting experiences from different areas of work and life, reflecting on how problems were solved, and noticing recurring structures beneath different surface details. When facing a new challenge, the person can search for analogous problems in other fields, identify the mechanism that made a previous solution effective, and ask whether the mechanism transfers. The person should then consult specialists, adapt the idea to local constraints and test it. Keeping a record of observations and experiments can make useful connections easier to recognize later. Brokerage is strengthened by curiosity, reflection, translation and disciplined testing.
Study instruction:
- "Keep a short idea-transfer journal for one week, recording a problem, an analogous solution from another setting and a testable adaptation."
Key point:
- Brokerage is developed through experience, reflection and deliberate transfer.
- A promising analogy must be adapted and tested in its new context.
52. Q: How can a team use its members' personal experiences without overgeneralizing from them?
A: Show answer
Personal experiences can reveal emotional and practical details that are easy to miss in abstract discussion, but one person's experience should not be treated as universal evidence. A team can use stories to generate hypotheses, identify overlooked needs and make a problem more concrete. It should then compare those insights with other people's experiences, available evidence and the intended audience's circumstances. The Frozen writers used their own feelings to make the characters more human, but they still needed to integrate the insights into a story that worked for viewers. Personal experience is a source of creative material, not a substitute for listening to others or testing assumptions.
Study instruction:
- "Use one personal experience to generate a design hypothesis, then list what additional perspectives or evidence would be needed before generalizing it."
Key point:
- Personal experience can generate insight but cannot represent every user or audience.
- Reflection should lead to broader inquiry and testing.
53. Q: How can managers recognize when a project is experiencing creative stagnation?
A: Show answer
Warning signs include repeated revisions that do not resolve the central problem, discussions that recycle the same arguments, strong attachment to existing work, reluctance to hear outside criticism and a narrowing range of options. Teams may appear busy while making little progress because they are repeatedly adjusting details without revisiting the underlying assumptions. A manager should ask whether the team can explain the project's core purpose, what evidence supports its current direction and which alternatives remain open. If members cannot view the work from another perspective, a targeted review or change in team dynamics may be needed.
Study instruction:
- "Create a stagnation checklist using repeated discussions, shrinking options, attachment to prior work and lack of progress on the central problem."
Key point:
- Activity is not the same as creative progress.
- Stagnation often signals a need for a different perspective or a changed process.
54. Q: How should a team decide whether to preserve or abandon an established idea?
A: Show answer
The decision should be based on the idea's contribution to the project's purpose, not on how long people have worked on it or how strongly they identify with it. The team should compare the idea with audience needs, available evidence, constraints and plausible alternatives. It should also distinguish a flawed core concept from a component that can be adapted. The chapter's examples show that familiar conventions can remain useful when recombined thoughtfully, while cherished elements may need to be changed when they prevent the whole work from succeeding. A disciplined decision process allows the team to preserve what works without protecting every previous choice.
Study instruction:
- "Evaluate one established project idea using purpose, evidence, audience value, adaptability and the cost of retaining it."
Key point:
- Retain ideas because they serve the purpose, not because they are familiar.
- An idea may be adapted or removed when it blocks the wider solution.
55. Q: How can innovation be evaluated when there is no universal formula for creativity?
A: Show answer
The chapter argues that creativity depends on novelty and surprise, so no checklist can guarantee an innovative result. Nevertheless, teams can evaluate whether a process is creating useful conditions and whether a proposed result solves the intended problem. They can examine the diversity of ideas considered, the quality of evidence, the usefulness of cross-domain connections, the response of intended users and the degree to which the final solution differs meaningfully from existing approaches. Evaluation should not reduce creativity to a single score. It should combine judgment about originality with practical evidence of relevance, coherence and effectiveness.
Study instruction:
- "Develop a balanced review rubric that considers novelty, coherence, usefulness, evidence and the response of intended users without claiming to measure creativity perfectly."
Key point:
- No universal checklist can guarantee originality.
- A combination of process indicators and practical outcome evidence can guide evaluation.
56. Q: What lessons can project teams draw from the way Frozen was revised under a tight deadline?
A: Show answer
The Frozen team faced a fixed release schedule, an unsatisfactory early draft and repeated failed attempts to solve the story. Progress came through candid critique, attention to personal experience, the discovery of a unifying idea and a carefully chosen change in team authority. The story was not rescued by simply working faster or adding more material. The team needed to identify what the film was really about and reconsider elements that did not support it. For other projects, the lesson is to combine urgency with structured feedback, reflection and permission to revise rather than allowing the deadline to lock the team into a weak solution.
Study instruction:
- "Compare your project's deadline pressure with the Frozen case and identify where critique, reflection or a change in perspective could improve the work."
Key point:
- A deadline alone does not create a successful solution.
- Candid review and a clear core can help a team use pressure productively.
57. Q: How can an idea broker test whether a transferred solution is appropriate for a new setting?
A: Show answer
The broker should first identify the mechanism that made the original solution effective and the conditions on which it depended. The receiving team should then compare those conditions with its own context, including users, resources, rules, risks and constraints. A small pilot can reveal whether the mechanism works, what needs adaptation and which side effects may occur. The broker should involve people who understand the receiving environment and should not claim that success elsewhere guarantees success locally. This approach preserves the value of cross-domain learning while preventing superficial copying.
Study instruction:
- "Prepare a transfer test with the original problem, mechanism, enabling conditions, local differences, pilot design and criteria for adoption."
Key point:
- Transfer the underlying mechanism, not just the visible form of a solution.
- Local testing is necessary because context can change the result.
58. Q: What is the relationship between creative flexibility and the willingness to change roles or power structures?
A: Show answer
The Frozen example suggests that a modest change in authority can alter how a team interprets information and makes decisions. Lee's promotion to co-director gave her a different responsibility and changed the way she listened to colleagues. It also disrupted a settled pattern without adding an entirely new group of participants. This does not mean that changing titles is a universal innovation technique. It shows that roles and power structures shape whose observations are heard, how feedback is interpreted and who feels responsible for defining the direction. Leaders should examine whether the current structure is preventing valuable perspectives from influencing the work.
Study instruction:
- "Map who has authority to speak, challenge and decide in a project, then identify whether a role adjustment could improve the use of existing expertise."
Key point:
- Roles influence what people notice and how their contributions are used.
- A targeted authority change can reopen a stuck decision process.
59. Q: How can creative teams preserve useful tension while maintaining collaboration?
A: Show answer
Teams can preserve useful tension by making disagreement about ideas acceptable while setting clear expectations for respect, evidence and shared purpose. Members should be able to challenge assumptions, question a preferred solution and explain concerns without personal attacks. Leaders can invite dissenting views, rotate reviewers and distinguish critique of the work from criticism of the person. At the same time, the team needs a process for making decisions so that disagreement does not become endless debate. The chapter's discussion of divergent thinking suggests that differences can expand options, while the Frozen case shows that teams still need a clear core and a decision when alternatives cannot all be retained.
Study instruction:
- "Write team norms that protect candid challenge while defining how evidence, respect, decision rights and closure will be handled."
Key point:
- Constructive tension keeps alternatives visible.
- Collaboration also requires clear decision processes and shared purpose.
60. Q: Why is it important to distinguish a creative process from a creative outcome?
A: Show answer
A team can use a productive process and still fail to produce a breakthrough because the result depends on uncertain factors such as timing, audience response and the quality of the idea itself. Conversely, a successful outcome may arise without a process that can be repeated reliably. The chapter's central argument is that the process can be improved even though creativity cannot be guaranteed. Teams should therefore evaluate both how they worked—whether they combined knowledge, invited critique and maintained flexibility—and what they produced. This distinction helps organizations learn from unsuccessful experiments without assuming that every process failure is proof that the idea was poor.
Study instruction:
- "Create separate measures for process quality and outcome quality, and explain how each would inform the next project."
Key point:
- A better process raises the likelihood of useful innovation but cannot guarantee it.
- Learning requires evaluating both how the team worked and what resulted.
61. Q: How can a leader encourage employees to look beyond their own department when solving a problem?
A: Show answer
A leader can frame the problem in terms of the underlying function rather than the department's usual procedures. Employees can be asked how similar problems are handled elsewhere in the organization or in other industries. The leader can arrange conversations with people who use different methods, invite them to demonstrate their approach and support a small test of the most promising transfer. This makes brokerage part of ordinary problem-solving rather than an occasional brainstorming event. Leaders should also recognize employees who share useful knowledge and ensure that cross-department collaboration has time and authority to influence decisions.
Study instruction:
- "Take one recurring departmental problem and identify three other teams or industries that may have solved a structurally similar problem."
Key point:
- Cross-boundary inquiry expands the range of available solutions.
- Leaders make brokerage more likely by enabling contact and experimentation.
62. Q: What does the chapter suggest about the role of anxiety and fear in the creative identity of innovators?
A: Show answer
The chapter presents anxiety as a possible signal that a person is confronting uncertainty and leaving familiar methods, not automatically as evidence of failure. Creative work often requires people to risk rejection, question established conventions and expose unfinished ideas to criticism. The Frozen team and Robbins's collaborators experienced pressure while searching for a solution. Yet the chapter does not romanticize severe distress or suggest that fear should be ignored. The practical lesson is to recognize discomfort, examine whether it reflects a meaningful challenge, and use reflection and support to keep working constructively rather than retreating to the safest familiar answer.
Study instruction:
- "Identify a current creative challenge that feels uncertain and distinguish useful discomfort from conditions that require a pause or additional support."
Key point:
- Fear can accompany the uncertainty of creative work.
- The goal is to work with manageable uncertainty, not to glorify harmful stress.
63. Q: How can a person become more sensitive to the creative value of everyday experiences?
A: Show answer
The chapter advises people to pay attention to how experiences make them think and feel. An everyday frustration, an unexpected interaction or a familiar task may reveal a problem that could be solved in a new way. The Post-it note example begins with an annoyance about bookmarks falling out of a hymnal; cellophane emerged from a desire to protect tablecloths; and infant formula is connected with a practical challenge faced by a parent. These examples suggest that observation becomes creative material when a person pauses to examine the underlying need rather than dismissing the experience as routine.
Study instruction:
- "Keep a daily observation log of frustrations, workarounds and surprising experiences, and identify the underlying need in each entry."
Key point:
- Ordinary experiences can reveal problems worth solving.
- Reflection turns passing observations into potential creative material.
64. Q: Why should innovators revisit established ideas when they feel creatively stuck?
A: Show answer
When people face a difficult problem, they may assume that the solution must be completely new. The chapter recommends looking again at what is already known and reconsidering conventions that have worked in other contexts. This can reduce the search space and provide building blocks for a new combination. Robbins used familiar theatrical techniques in unexpected ways, while the Frozen team drew on familiar emotional experiences to reshape a fairy-tale story. Revisiting established ideas is not a retreat from originality; it is a way to find components that can be recombined and adapted to the problem at hand.
Study instruction:
- "When facing a difficult task, list five established methods from other settings and assess which underlying principles could transfer."
Key point:
- Existing knowledge provides material for new combinations.
- A fresh context can make an established idea useful in a different way.
65. Q: What are the three practical lessons the chapter gives to people who want to become innovation brokers?
A: Show answer
The chapter identifies three connected practices. First, be sensitive to personal experiences and reflect on how they make you think and feel; this helps distinguish genuine insight from cliché. Second, recognize that creative stress or desperation can sometimes increase flexibility and encourage a new look at old ideas. Third, preserve critical distance after a breakthrough by challenging what has been created, seeking different perspectives and adjusting team dynamics when necessary. Together, these practices help people notice useful connections, tolerate uncertainty and avoid becoming trapped by their own successful ideas.
Study instruction:
- "Create a personal development plan with one practice for reflective observation, one for handling creative pressure and one for critical review."
Key point:
- Reflection, constructive engagement with uncertainty and critical distance reinforce one another.
- Innovation brokerage is a practice that can be developed over time.
66. Q: How does the chapter connect individual creativity with organizational design?
A: Show answer
The chapter moves between individual habits and the structures in which people work. Individuals need varied experiences, reflection and willingness to reconsider familiar ideas. Organizations can support those habits by connecting groups, allowing employees to contribute ideas, arranging candid review and changing roles when a team becomes stuck. The Toyota example highlights employee control, while the Disney case shows how authority and listening shaped the use of existing perspectives. Innovation is therefore neither purely an individual trait nor simply a product of organizational policy. It emerges through interaction between people's knowledge and the conditions that allow it to be shared and challenged.
Study instruction:
- "Assess an organization at both the individual and structural levels, identifying what helps or prevents employees from contributing and combining ideas."
Key point:
- Individual habits and organizational conditions work together.
- Structures can enable or suppress the exchange and development of ideas.
67. Q: What are the main conclusions to draw from Chapter 7, “Innovation: How Idea Brokers and Creative Desperation Saved Disney’s Frozen”?
A: Show answer
The chapter argues that innovation often emerges when people combine familiar ideas in unusual ways, connect knowledge across groups and use personal experience as material for creative work. The examples of scientific papers, Edison, IDEO, West Side Story and Frozen show different forms of brokerage and recombination. The chapter also explains that pressure can sometimes loosen habitual thinking, while too much comfort and attachment can cause a team to spin in place. Connell's intermediate disturbance hypothesis provides an analogy for the value of proportionate disruption. The final lessons are to reflect on experience, use manageable creative tension and preserve critical distance so that successful ideas do not crowd out better alternatives.
Study instruction:
- "Review the chapter's research, case studies and three concluding practices, then explain how they connect into one approach to developing innovation."
Key point:
- Innovation grows through recombination, brokerage and reflective use of experience.
- Manageable tension and critical distance help keep creative alternatives alive.
